Fused K-operators and the q q -Onsager algebra
Guillaume Lemarthe, Pascal Baseilhac, Azat M. Gainutdinov

Abstract
. We study universal solutions to reflection equations with a spectral parameter, so-called K-operators, within a general framework of universal K-matrices – an extended version of the approach introduced by Appel-Vlaar. Here, the input data is a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra H H , its comodule algebra B B and a pair of consistent twists. In our setting, the universal K-matrix is an element of B ⊗ H B\otimes H satisfying certain axioms, and we consider the case H = ℒ ​ U q ​ s ​ l 2 H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2} , the quantum loop algebra for s ​ l 2 sl_{2} , and B = 𝒜 q B=\mathcal{A}_{q} , the alternating central extension of the q q -Onsager algebra. Considering tensor products of evaluation representations of ℒ ​ U q ​ s ​ l 2 \mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2} in “non-semisimple” cases, the new set of axioms allows us to introduce and study fused K-operators of spin- j j ; in particular, to prove that for all j ∈ 1 2 ​ ℕ j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N} they satisfy the spectral-parameter dependent reflection equation. We provide their explicit expression in terms of elements of the algebra 𝒜 q {\cal A}_{q} for small values of spin- j j . The precise relation between the fused K-operators of spin- j j and evaluations of a universal K-matrix for 𝒜 q {\cal A}_{q} is conjectured based on supporting evidences. Independently, we study K-operator solutions of the twisted intertwining relations associated with the comodule algebra 𝒜 q {\cal A}_{q} , and expand them in the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt basis of 𝒜 q {\cal A}_{q} . With a reasonably general ansatz, we found a unique solution for first few values of j j which agrees with the fused K-operators, as expected. We conjecture that in general such solutions are uniquely determined and match with the expressions of the fused K-operators.



MSC: 81R50; 81R10; 81U15.

Keywords
: Reflection equation; Universal K-matrix;
q
-Onsager algebra; Fusion for K-operators

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

In the context of quantum integrable systems, the R- and K-matrices are the basic ingredients for the construction of the monodromy matrix (or its double row version) leading to the generating function for mutually commuting quantities, the so-called transfer matrix. Here, the formal variable of the generating function is called ‘spectral parameter’, denoted by
u
. By definition, the R-matrix is a solution of the Yang-Baxter equation with this spectral parameter, whereas the K-matrix satisfies a reflection equation, also known under the name boundary Yang-Baxter equation.
For a triple of finite-dimensional vector spaces
V^{(j_{k})}
, for
k=1,2,3
, the corresponding Yang-Baxter equation in
\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{1})}\otimes V^{(j_{2})}\otimes V^{(j_{3})})
takes the form
[
Ya67
,
Ba72
]
:

(1.1)

R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}_{12}(u_{1}/u_{2})R^{(j_{1},j_{3})}_{13}(u_{1}/u_{3})R^{(j_{2},j_{3})}_{23}(u_{2}/u_{3})=R^{(j_{2},j_{3})}_{23}(u_{2}/u_{3})R^{(j_{1},j_{3})}_{13}(u_{1}/u_{3})R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}_{12}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

where
u_{k}
are the spectral parameters, and
R^{(j_{n},j_{m})}_{nm}(u)
are the R-matrices on corresponding products of spaces:

R_{12}=R\otimes{\mathbb{I}}
,
R_{23}={\mathbb{I}}\otimes R
,
R_{13}=(\cal P\otimes{\mathbb{I}})R_{23}({\cal P}\otimes{\mathbb{I}})
,
with
\cal P(a\otimes b)=b\otimes a
.
Given an R-matrix
R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
satisfying (
1.1
), the reflection equation in
\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{1})}\otimes V^{(j_{2})})
is given by
[
C84
,
Sk88
]

(1.2)

\displaystyle R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})

\displaystyle{K}^{(j_{1})}_{1}(u_{1})R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}u_{2}){K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})=

\displaystyle{K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2}){K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

where

(1.3)

R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)=\mathcal{P}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}R^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)\mathcal{P}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}\ .

Along the years, several examples of solutions to (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) have been obtained for the case of 2 or 3-dimensional spaces
V^{(j_{k})}
, see e.g.
[
ZZ79
,
ZF80
,
GZ93
,
VG93
,
IOZ96
]
. Interestingly, these
4\times 4
R-matrices can be interpreted as intertwining operators for underlying action of the quantum affine algebra
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
on tensor product of so-called evaluation representations of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
, or briefly these are spin-
\frac{1}{2}
solutions (and similarly for the 3-dimensional or spin-1 solutions). The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
expressions of the K-matrix are interpreted analogously as ‘twisted’ intertwiners (with respect to the spectral parameter ‘reflection’
u\to u^{-1}
) for the action of a coideal subalgebra of the quantum affine algebra.

For higher values of spins
j_{k}
, constructing R- and K-matrices by brute force is increasingly complicated. To circumvent this problem, two different methods have been proposed that are summarized as follows (both methods use the underlying action of the quantum affine algebra):

(i)

Fusion method:
The R- and K-matrices for higher spins are derived using a fusion procedure. This procedure for the R-matrix has been originally developed in
[
Ka79
,
KRS81
,
KR87
]
, and for the K-matrix in
[
MN92
]
. For a more recent approach, see
[
RSV14
,
BLN15
,
NP15
]
. Starting from R- and K-matrix solutions of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
, the fused R- and K-matrices of spin-
j
are obtained inductively by tensoring (or “fusing”) the fundamental representations of
U_{q}sl_{2}
and projecting onto the highest spin sub-representation.

(ii)

Intertwining method:
The idea is to demand that R-matrices for higher spins satisfy a set of intertwining relations with respect to the quantum affine algebra action on
V^{(j_{1})}\otimes V^{(j_{2})}
, given by its coproduct.
And similarly for the K-matrix, it should satisfy a twisted intertwining relation for the action of a certain coideal subalgebra in the quantum affine algebra.
According to the representation chosen, the twisted intertwining relations lead to a set of recurrence relations for the (scalar) entries of the R- and K-matrices. This technique has been initiated in
[
KR83
]
for the R-matrix and in
[
MN97
,
DM01
]
for the K-matrix where the coideal subalgebra is now known as the
q
-Onsager algebra
O_{q}

[
T99
,
B04
]
.

For instance, for the R-matrix associated with
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
that enjoys P-symmetry
[
RSV14
]

(1.4)

R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)=R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)\ ,

the corresponding fused solutions using method (i) have been constructed in
[
KS82
]
, while Intertwining method (ii) was implemented in
[
KR83
]
.
For the K-matrix
{K}^{(j)}(u)
associated with
O_{q}
(a coideal subalgebra of
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
), the fusion method (i) for spin-
1
was implemented in
[
IOZ96
]
, while the method (ii) for an arbitrary spin was used in
[
DN02
]
to produce recurrence relations for entries of the K-matrix. We also note that potentially related recurrence relations for higher-spin K-matrix were also obtained in
[
ML19
]
.

It is well-known
[
Dr86
]
that the Yang-Baxter equation (
1.1
) can be derived from the more general setting of Yang-Baxter algebras and the universal Yang-Baxter equation, see
[
DF93
,
JLM19a
,
JLM19b
]
, by specialization to the finite-dimensional representations
V^{(j_{k})}
that depend on the spectral parameter
u
. Namely, R-matrix solutions of (
1.1
) are obtained by specializing L-operators of the form

(1.5)

\widehat{{\bf L}}^{(j)}(u)\in H\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j)})\

that satisfy an equation in
H\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{1})}\otimes V^{(j_{2})})
:

(1.6)

R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u/v)\widehat{{\bf L}}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u)\widehat{{\bf L}}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(v)=\widehat{{\bf L}}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(v)\widehat{{\bf L}}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u)R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u/v)\ .

Here
H
is assumed to be any quantum affine algebra and
V^{(j)}
’s are also known as evaluation representations.
Strictly speaking, the first component of
\widehat{{\bf L}}^{(j)}(u)
belongs to
H((u))
, the algebra of formal Laurent series in
u
with coefficients in
H
.
For simplicity, in the introduction we often indicate just an algebra like
H
instead of

H((u))
.

Furthermore, the L-operators themselves can be obtained by specializing 2nd component of the universal R-matrix
\mathfrak{R}\in H\otimes H
that is known to exist in an appropriate completion of the tensor product. For instance, for
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
the universal R-matrix is expressed as an infinite product of
q
-exponentials
involving the root vectors
[
KT92a
,
LSS93
,
Da98
]
1
1

1

Strictly speaking, the universal R-matrices introduced in these works are for quantised Kac–Moody algebras, extensions of
U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
by a derivation. However, in our work we will not make any distinction between quantised affine and Kac–Moody algebras, as all representations we consider are of zero central charge
which means that we actually work with their quotients called quantum loop algebras.
. The evaluation of
\mathfrak{R}
on finite-dimensional representations requires to treat
u
as a formal parameter to avoid convergence problems. This was suggested in
[
Dr86
, Sec. 13]
and later studied for
H=U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
in
[
FR92
, Sec. 4]
, see
[
He17
, Sec. 1]
for a review.

In the framework of the universal R-matrix, the previously existing methods (i) and (ii) find a natural interpretation: they just follow from the universal R-matrix axioms (
R1
)-(
R3
) reviewed in Section
2.1
. As expected, expressions for the R-matrices previously derived in the literature using Fusion method (i) and Intertwining method (ii) match, up to a scalar function, with those derived through the specializations of the universal R-matrix.

For the reflection equation, it is well known
[
Sk88
]
that (
1.2
) has a representation-theoretic interpretation within the more general framework of reflection algebras, which are generated by the modes of the matrix entries of so-called
K-operators

(1.7)

\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)\in B\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j)})\ ,

satisfying for all
j
the following reflection equations in
B\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{1})}\otimes V^{(j_{2})})
:
2
2

2

In the case of P-symmetric R-matrices associated to
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
, the reflection equation (
1.8
) for the K-operators takes the more standard form (
4.67
).

(1.8)

\displaystyle R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(j_{1})}_{1}(u_{1})R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}u_{2})\mathcal{K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2})\mathcal{K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})R_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ .

Here
B
is in general assumed to be a comodule algebra over
H
, a notion generalizing coideal subalgebras in
H
.
3
3

3

There are also K-operators that might not fit our setting. For instance, the K-operators associated with a
q
-oscillator algebra or the Askey-Wilson algebra were constructed in
[
BK02
,
B04
]
respectively, see also
[
BF11
]
for quotients of a few higher rank generalizations of
O_{q}
. However, we are not aware of any comodule algebra structure for these algebras.

In other words, unlike K-matrices, which have scalar entries, K-operators are matrices with entries belonging to
B
.

In this paper we consider
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
with zero central charge, so-called quantum loop algebra, and the comodule algebra of primary interest for us is
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
, the alternating central extension
[
BS09
,
T21a
]
of the
q
-Onsager algebra
O_{q}
. Our main motivation in this comodule algebra comes from applications to open spin-chains through the K-operators, as it will be discussed below and in more detail in
[
LBG24
]
. It is important to note that we do not have any explicit form of a K-operator for
O_{q}
, while it is known for the comodule algebra
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}

[
BS09
]
in the fundamental case
j=\frac{1}{2}
. Moreover, with this K-operator
\mathcal{K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
the reflection equation (
1.8
) for
j_{1}=j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
is the sole
defining
relation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. We thus have a very compact presentation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
which is analogous to the famous FRT presentation
[
DF93
]
of
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
.

The K-operators can also produce their smaller cousins—the K-matrices—by evaluating one-dimensional representations of
B
on the 1st tensor component of
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
.
Indeed, we show in
[
LBG24
]
that one-dimensional representations of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
produce out of the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
K-operator
\mathcal{K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
the most general K-matrix of the open XXZ spin-
\frac{1}{2}
chain
[
dVR93
,
GZ93
]
. Furthermore, on tensor-product representations of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
, the K-operator
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
images match with ‘dressed’ K-matrices or Sklyanin’s operators, and the important problem of functional relations between transfer matrices of different spins, the so-called
TT-relations
, can be investigated at the level of algebraic relations of
{\cal A}_{q}
, as we discuss in more detail in Section
1.4
. The main goal of our program is to construct such a general framework that allows the development of universal TT-relations holding in
\mathcal{A}_{q}
, which would then specialize to the known (conjectured) TT-relations for transfer matrices in the literature such as
[
FNR07
,
CYSW14
]
.
This paper provides the first step in this direction by developing a formalism of K-operators with the spectral parameter, as we discuss it now.

1.2.
Universal K-matrix and comodule algebras

As it was with the L-operators, it is expected that the K-operators
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
from (
1.7
) can arise from a universal K-matrix satisfying a universal reflection equation.
The concept of a universal K-matrix is not new, it has been studied originally in
[
CG92
,
KSS92
,
tDHO98
,
DKM02
]
. More recently, significant progress has been made in the works of
[
BW13
,
BKo15
,
Ko17
,
AV20
]
.
Each of the definitions of universal K-matrices introduced in these recent references has
different axioms that were chosen to serve different purposes. We can highlight three directions
corresponding to where a universal K-matrix belongs to:

(1)

H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, in
[
BKo15
]
;

(2)

B\otimes H
, where
B
is a coideal subalgebra of
H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, in
[
Ko17
]
;

(3)

H=U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
, with the data of a certain automorphism of
H
, called ‘twist’, in
[
AV20
]
.

Let us clarify that (1) and (3) imply a choice of
B
as a coideal subalgebra over
H
to form a quantum symmetric pair
(H,B)
, and the universal K-matrix satisfies intertwining relations, twisted in (3), with respect to
B
(and not
H
as the R-matrix).

Let us start with direction (2). Kolb introduced a universal K-matrix
\mathscr{K}\in B\otimes H
that satisfies certain axioms
[
Ko17
, Def. 2.7]
:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}\Delta_{B}(b)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{B}(b)\mathscr{K}\ ,\qquad\text{for all $b\in B$},

\displaystyle(\Delta_{B}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathscr{K})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{32}\mathscr{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ ,

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathscr{K})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{32}\mathscr{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\mathscr{K}_{12}\ .

It satisfies an algebraic (non-matrix) version of the reflection equation that belongs to
B\otimes H\otimes H

\mathfrak{R}_{32}\mathscr{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\mathscr{K}_{12}=\mathscr{K}_{12}\mathfrak{R}_{32}\mathscr{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ .

The specialization of its second tensor component in
H
, to a finite-dimensional representation leads to K-operators that do not depend on a spectral parameter, denoted as
\mathcal{K}\in B\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j)})
, satisfying a reflection equation of the form

(1.10)

R_{12}\mathcal{K}_{1}R_{21}\mathcal{K}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{2}R_{12}\mathcal{K}_{1}R_{21}\ ,

which belongs to
B\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{1})})\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j_{2})})
. Moreover, the evaluation of the second relation in eq. (
1.9
) takes the form

(1.11)

(\Delta_{B}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathcal{K})=\big(\mathcal{L}\big)_{[2]}\big(\mathcal{K}\big)_{[1]}\big(\mathcal{L}\big)_{[2]}\ .

Next, let us clarify that direction (1) is a special case of (2). Indeed, applying the counit to the first component of
\mathscr{K}
, we obtain an object
k\in H
that was introduced by Balagović and Kolb in
[
BKo15
]
. Its specialization to a finite-dimensional representation leads to K-matrices (with scalar entries).

However, axiomatics of directions (1) and (2) is not suitable for the reflection equation with spectral parameter (
1.2
). To change this, Appel and Vlaar proposed in
[
AV20
]
a twisted version of the approach (1) in
[
BKo15
]
, this is direction (3). Their definition of universal K-matrix is associated with a coideal subalgebra of
H=U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
and includes a pair of consistent twists.
It was shown in
[
AV20
]
that such a universal K-matrix exists for a large class of coideals known as quantum symmetric pairs. Furthermore, it was shown in
[
AV22
]
that these universal K-matrices are well-defined on finite-dimensional evaluation representations of
H=U_{q}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
and provide K-matrix solutions of (
1.2
).
Unfortunately, no explicit expression of these universal K-matrices is known even in simplest cases, and the existence result requires a specific form of twists.

Furthermore, whereas the axiomatics and results of
[
AV20
,
AV22
]
are well-suited for coideal subalgebras like
O_{q}
, they cannot be used for the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. Indeed,
\mathcal{A}_{q}
is an extension of
O_{q}
by an infinite number of algebraically independent central elements and, to the best of our knowledge, it cannot be realized as a coideal subalgebra of
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}

or any other quasi-triangular Hopf algebra.
We therefore cannot use the axiomatics and results of
[
AV20
,
AV22
]
, and it requires an adjustment in order to treat general comodule algebras like
\mathcal{A}_{q}
.

In this paper, to produce K-operator solutions of (
1.8
), we combine the approaches (2) and (3), and introduce a twisted version
\mathfrak{K}\in B\otimes H
of the universal K-matrix from
[
Ko17
]
via a new set of axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
) in Section
2.3
that generalize those in (
1.9
) and at the same time recover the approach (3) via the counit application. The notion of a twist
\psi
(certain automorphism of
H
) is important here because, as it will be discussed in Section
4
, a natural choice of
\psi
allows the derivation of the K-operator reflection equation (
1.8
) from the
\psi
-twisted universal reflection equation satisfied by
\mathfrak{K}
and given in Proposition
2.11
. We also note that here
B
is not assumed to be a coideal in
H
, in particular we do not assume
(H,B)
to be a quantum symmetric pair, but
B
is a right comodule algebra over
H
.

We conjecture that such a universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}
for our choice of
\psi=\eta
defined in (
2.34
) exists,
4
4

4

In the context of 1-component universal K-matrix
k\in H
, there are existence results for other choices of twists in
[
AV22
]
. We discuss this properly at the end of Section
2
.
so that its 2nd component evaluation on
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
representations leads to the known examples of K-operators (that can be further evaluated to get known examples of K-matrices
[
LBG24
]
).
However, the examples of K-operators are only known for the fundamental or ‘spin’-
\frac{1}{2}
representation, the
\mathcal{K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
for
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
mentioned above. It is thus natural to investigate further, along the directions (i) or (ii), the spectral parameter dependent K-operators for arbitrary spin representations.
The universal K-matrix axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
) provide the main guiding directions for such investigation, as it was the case for the interpretation of these methods for L-operators and R-matrices.

1.3.
Goal and main results

The purpose of this paper is to construct K-operators with a spectral parameter for arbitrary spin representations and show how they relate to specializations of a universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}\in B\otimes H
.
For this, we develop the directions (i) and (ii) further to the case of K-operators (
1.7
) based on our new set of axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
), and apply them in the case of the comodule algebra
B={\cal A}_{q}
.
The main results are the following:

(I)

Universal fusion method:
Using the axiom (
K2
) and the detailed analysis of tensor product representation of
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
, we derive
fused
K-operators
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
for arbitrary values of spin-
j
, starting from the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
K-operator, see Definition
5.6
based on the result in Proposition
4.16
. One of our main results (Theorem
5.7
) is that they satisfy the reflection equations (
1.8
) for all
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
and with no assumption made on existence of
\mathfrak{K}
. For
j=1,\frac{3}{2}
, we give explicit expressions of the fused K-operators in terms of generating functions of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
.

(II)

Universal intertwining method:

K-operators that solve the twisted intertwining relations for
B={\cal A}_{q}
are considered in Section
7
.
These relations, see (
5.55
), are just specialization of the axiom (
K1
).
For
j=\frac{1}{2},1
and with a reasonably general ansatz of
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
, solutions of the twisted intertwining relations are obtained using a Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt (PBW) basis of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. They are found to be uniquely determined (up to an overall factor), and they match with the fundamental K-operator and the spin-
1
fused K-operator.
Then, it is conjectured that the twisted intertwining relations determine uniquely the K-operators for any
j
and that they match with the fused K-operators constructed through the method (I).

Let us comment more on constructions behind the Universal fusion method (I).
Drawing inspiration from the evaluation of one of the axioms of the universal K-matrix, and
without making any assumptions about the existence of
\mathfrak{K}
, we define
fused spin-
j
K-operators
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)\in\mathcal{A}_{q}((u^{-1}))\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
through the following recursion based on the spin-
1/2
K-operator from the reflection algebra presentation of
{\cal A}_{q}
in Theorem
5.1
:

(1.12)

\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{K}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j+\frac{1}{2}})R^{(\frac{1}{2},j-\frac{1}{2})}(u^{2}q^{-j+1})\mathcal{K}_{2}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

and that we call
universal fusion
procedure.
Here, we used
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
-intertwiners

\mathcal{E}^{(j)}\colon\mathbb{C}_{u}^{2j+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j}\ ,\quad u_{1}=uq^{-j+\frac{1}{2}},\quad u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

for evaluation representations, and we denote their pseudo-inverse maps

\mathcal{F}^{(j)}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}_{u_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{u}^{2j+1}
.
The conditions imposed on
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
ensure that the tensor product of the evaluation representations of
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
admits a spin-
j
sub-representation (which is not a direct summand). This reducibility condition is expressed in terms of ratio of the evaluation parameters
[
CP91
, Sect. 4.9]
.
Using this criterion, we explicitly construct
\mathcal{E}^{(j)}
and
\mathcal{F}^{(j)}
by determining their matrix expressions, as described in Section
3.2
.

Inspiration for the above universal fusion (
1.12
) was taken from fusion relations in Proposition
4.16
satisfied by the spin-
j
K-operator

(1.13)

{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)=({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j})(\mathfrak{K})\ \in\ B((u^{-1}))\otimes\text{\rm End}(V^{(j)})\ .

obtained from the 1-component evaluation of
\mathfrak{K}
.
Here, the universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}
is considered for the pair of twists
(\psi,J)=(\eta,1\otimes 1)
with
\eta
defined in (
2.34
).
Moreover, a careful analysis requires treating
u
as a formal variable,
and the representation
\pi^{j}_{u}\colon H\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(V^{(j)}[u^{\pm 1}])
used in (
1.13
) is what we call
formal evaluation
representation of
H=U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
defined in Section
3.5
. It is an infinite-dimensional analogue of the finite-dimensional evaluation representations of
H
. These representations of quantum affine algebras are not of highest weight type, and also known under the name ‘quantum loop modules’
[
CG03
]
.
In Section
3.5
, we also analyze tensor products of the formal evaluation representations
\pi^{j}_{u}
and obtain the intertwining operators
\mathcal{E}^{(j)}
and their pseudo-inverses
\mathcal{F}^{(j)}
in this framework with
u
being a formal variable.

It is important to note that, in contrast to the situation with L-operators, there is no single example in the literature of an evaluation (
1.13
), even for
j=\frac{1}{2}
.
On the other side, the formula (
1.12
) gives explicit expressions for K-operators.
Furthermore, we propose a precise relation that links the K-operators
{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)
in (
1.13
) and the fused spin-
j
K-operators
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
in (
1.12
) associated with
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. This is stated in Conjecture
1
: the two K-operators equal up to a central and invertible element in
\mathcal{A}_{q}((u^{-1}))
, which is defined by a functional relation and has a specific coaction.
This conjecture is supported by showing independently that the fused K-operators (
1.12
) satisfy a set of relations arising from the evaluation of the axioms of the universal K-matrix.

1.4.
K-operators for
{\cal A}_{q}
and universal TT-relations

A construction of arbitrary spin K-operators for
{\cal A}_{q}
is not only important for a better understanding of the universal K-matrix formalism for comodule algebras, but first of all because of applications in quantum integrable systems. Namely, as will be reported in
[
LBG24
]
, a generating function
of a commutative subalgebra of
{\cal A}_{q}
can be constructed using the spin-
j
K-operator
{\mathcal{K}}^{(j)}(u)
, that can be viewed as a spin-
j

universal
transfer matrix
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)\in\mathcal{A}_{q}((u^{-1}))
. A family of
universal
TT-relations between the universal transfer matrices can be calculated directly in the algebra
{\cal A}_{q}
:

(1.14)

{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)={\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}){\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j-\frac{1}{2}})+f^{(j)}(u){\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j-1)}(uq^{-1})\ ,

with
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(0)}(u)\equiv 1
,
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(-\frac{1}{2})}(u)\equiv 0
and
f^{(j)}(u)
is a generating function of central elements in
\mathcal{A}_{q}
that is explicitly calculated
[
LBG24
]
. Considering
tensor product (or spin-chain) representations of
the algebra
B={\cal A}_{q}
, one can show that the K-operators
{\mathcal{K}}^{(j)}(u)
and the universal transfer matrices
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
map, respectively, to the ‘dressed’ K-matrices and transfer matrices associated with various examples of integrable spin chains of XXZ type with generic boundary conditions. Importantly, whereas
f^{(j)}(u)
in (
1.14
) is universal, its images with respect to the various
q
-Onsager quotients associated with the spin chains are not:

the boundary conditions of the open integrable spin chains dictate what value the image of
f^{(j)}(u)
takes.

In other words, the algebra
{\cal A}_{q}
in a certain sense governs all known integrable boundary conditions through its quotients and degenerate cases
[
BB16
]
. Thus, many properties of the integrable models can be studied even before specializations to the spin-chain representations of
{\cal A}_{q}
, and so the K-operators of
{\cal A}_{q}
can be used in the
representation-independent
analysis of the related integrable models.
The applications of our results to integrable models are discussed more in Section
8
and in our forthcoming paper
[
LBG24
]
.

The text is organized as follows. In Section
2
, the formalism of universal R-matrix is reviewed following
[
Dr86
]
. Furthermore, we upgrade the universal K-matrix axioms of
[
Ko17
,
AV20
]
in Definition
2.8
. In our framework, the K-operators are obtained as evaluations of the universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}\in B\otimes H
(if it exists) satisfying a

\psi
-twisted universal reflection equation, see Proposition
2.11
.
In this section, we also review basic definitions of the quantum loop algebra
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, the
q
-Onsager algebra
O_{q}
and its alternating central extension
B={\cal A}_{q}
.

In Sections
3
-
5
,
we develop
Universal fusion method (I)
. Namely, Section
3
is devoted to a detailed analysis of the tensor product of evaluation representations of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and their formal-parameter versions.
In Section
4
, we assume existence of a universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}
for a comodule algebra
B
and a certain twist pair.
The L-operators and K-operators of spin-
j
are defined as formal evaluations of
\mathfrak{R}
and
\mathfrak{K}
, respectively; see Definitions
4.1
and
4.15
.
The main results of this section are the so-called ‘fusion’ relations satisfied by the L- and K-operators of spin-
j
, see Propositions
4.5
and
4.16
, respectively.
In Section
5
, we do not assume existence of a universal K-matrix for
{\cal A}_{q}
, and instead
introduce
fused K-operators
of spin-
j
in Definition
5.6
by a recursion based on the fundamental K-operator giving the reflection algebra presentation of
{\cal A}_{q}
. Theorem
5.7
shows that they satisfy the reflection equation (
1.8
)
for all choices of
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
. Explicit expressions of the fused K-operators for
j=1,\frac{3}{2}
are derived in Section
5.4
.
We also give compact expressions for the fused R-matrices and the fused K-operators in (
5.43
), (
5.44
), solely in terms of the fundamental R-matrix and K-operator.

In Section
6
, the precise relation between the spin-
j
K-operators from Section
4
and the fused K-operators from Section
5
leads to Conjecture
1
, with a few supporting evidences discussed.

In Section
7
,
Universal intertwining method (II)
is considered, and K-operator solutions of the twisted intertwining relations in Proposition
5.13

are investigated without any additional assumption.
Finally, in the concluding Section
8
, we give a brief summary of our results and discuss a few perspectives of applications of the K-operator formalism to quantum integrable systems.

In Appendix
A
, we recall the basics about the quantum algebras
U_{q}sl_{2}
and
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
.
In Appendix
B
, we adapt the universal R-matrix constructed by Khoroshkin-Tolstoy in
[
KT92a
]
to our conventions.
The corresponding Ding-Frenkel type L-operators
\widehat{{\bf L}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)={\bf L}^{\pm}(u)
and the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
are computed by evaluation of the universal R-matrix.
In Appendix
C
, we give ordering relations for the generating functions of
{\cal A}_{q}
.
Finally, in Appendix
D
, we give a proof of one of our main results, Theorem
5.7
, that the fused K-operators satisfy the reflection equations for any pair of spins.

Notations.

We denote the set of natural numbers by
{\mathbb{N}}=\{0
,
1
,
2
,
\ldots\}
and the positive integers by
\mathbb{N}_{+}=\{1
,
2
,
\ldots\}
.

All algebras are considered over the field of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, if not stated otherwise. Though the results till Section
2.4
are valid also over general fields, and many results in Section
5
can be directly generalized to algebraically closed fields of zero characteristic, we fix for simplicity the ground field to be
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and we assume in this paper that
q
is not a root of unity. The
q
-commutator is

\big[X,Y\big]_{q}=qXY-q^{-1}YX

and
\big[X,Y\big]=\big[X,Y\big]_{1}=XY-YX
.
We denote the
q
-numbers by
[n]_{q}=(q^{n}-q^{-n})/(q-q^{-1})
.

We denote by
\mathbb{I}_{2j}
the
2j\times 2j
identity matrix. We also use Pauli matrices:

(1.15)

\sigma_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

All generating functions considered in this paper like quantum determinants
\gamma(u)
or
\Gamma(u)
are formal Laurent series in
u^{-1}
with coefficients in the corresponding algebra like
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. In other words, they are of the form

\displaystyle{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}f_{n}u^{-n}\ ,

where
f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
and all but finitely many
f_{n}
for
n<0
vanish. In this case, we use the notation
\mathcal{A}_{q}((u^{-1}))
, and the convention that every rational function of the form
1/p(u)
, where
p(u)
is a Laurent polynomial, is expanded in
u^{-1}
. The space of formal power series with coefficients
f_{n}
in
\mathcal{A}_{q}
, i.e. when all
f_{n}
are zero for
n<0
, is denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{q}[[u^{-1}]]
.

2.
Universal R and K matrices

Firstly, we recall the definition of a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra
H
with the associated universal R-matrix that satisfies the universal Yang-Baxter equation. Then, inspired by the works
[
Ko17
]
and
[
AV20
]
, we define in Section
2.3
a universal K-matrix associated with
H
, a pair of its consistent twists
(\psi,J)
, and its comodule algebra
B
– this is an element in
B\otimes H
that satisfies a universal reflection equation (also called
\psi
-twisted reflection equation), see Proposition
2.11
. We also show a precise relation of our notion of the universal K-matrix to those introduced in
[
Ko17
]
and
[
AV20
]
. In Section
2.4
, we introduce the main example of
H
and
B
considered in this paper: the quantum loop algebra
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and its comodule algebra
B={\cal A}_{q}
, the alternating central extension of the
q
-Onsager algebra.

2.1.
Universal R-matrix

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra with coproduct
\Delta\colon H\rightarrow H\otimes H
, the counit
\epsilon\colon H\to\mathbb{C}
and the antipode
S\colon H\to H
, which are subject to consistency conditions, for which we refer to
[
CP95
, Chap. 4]
. We denote the opposite coproduct
\Delta^{op}=\mathfrak{p}\circ\Delta
, where
\mathfrak{p}
is the permutation operator, i.e.
\mathfrak{p}(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x
, for
x,y\in H
.
For
\mathfrak{R}\in H\otimes H,
we use the notation
\mathfrak{R}_{12}=\mathfrak{R}\otimes 1
,
\mathfrak{R}_{23}=1\otimes\mathfrak{R}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{13}=\mathfrak{p}_{23}\circ\mathfrak{R}_{12}
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
Dr86
]
)
.

For a Hopf algebra
H
, an invertible element
\mathfrak{R}\in H\otimes H
is called universal R-matrix if it satisfies

(R1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\Delta(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{op}(x)\mathfrak{R}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in H\ ,

(R2)

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ ,

(R3)

\displaystyle\ ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{R})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{12}\ .

If such
\mathfrak{R}
exists, then the pair
(H,\mathfrak{R})
is called a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra.

We note that the universal R-matrix necessarily satisfies

(2.1)

\displaystyle(S\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R})=\mathfrak{R}

{}^{-1}=({\text{id}}\otimes S)(\mathfrak{R})\ ,

(2.2)

\displaystyle(\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R})=1

\displaystyle=({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathfrak{R})\ .

Using the relations (
R1
)–(
R3
) one can show that the universal R-matrix satisfies the universal Yang-Baxter equation:

(2.3)

\mathfrak{R}_{12}\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}=\mathfrak{R}_{23}\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{12}\ .

It is well-known
[
Dr86
]
that the universal R-matrix coming from a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra gives a way to generate R-matrices on tensor product of representations, via evaluations as we will see in Section
4
.

2.2.
Twist pairs

The following is an extension of
[
AV20
, Def. 2.2]
from bialgebras to Hopf algebras.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(H,\mathfrak{R})
be a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra and
\psi\colon H\rightarrow H
an algebra automorphism. The
\psi
-twisting of
(H,\mathfrak{R})
is the quasi-triangular Hopf algebra
(H^{\psi},\mathfrak{R}^{\psi\psi})
obtained from
(H,\mathfrak{R})
by pullback through
\psi
, i.e.
H^{\psi}
is the Hopf algebra with same multiplication, new coproduct, counit and antipode
5
5

5

It is enough to verify that
\epsilon^{\psi}\circ S^{\psi}=\epsilon^{\psi}
.
:

(2.4)

\Delta^{\psi}=(\psi\otimes\psi)\circ\Delta\circ\psi^{-1},\qquad\epsilon^{\psi}=\epsilon\circ\psi^{-1},\qquad S^{\psi}=\psi\circ S\circ\psi^{-1}\ ,

and the universal R-matrix is given by

(2.5)

\mathfrak{R}^{\psi\psi}=(\psi\otimes\psi)(\mathfrak{R})\ .

In what follows, we also use the opposite version of the
\psi
-twisting of
(H,\mathfrak{R})
. Let
H^{cop}
be the Hopf algebra with the coproduct
\Delta^{op}
, the antipode
S^{-1}
and the other structure maps are the same as
H
. Let
H^{cop,\psi}
be the
\psi
-twisting of
H^{cop}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The pair
(H^{cop,\psi},\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})
is a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra.

Proof.

We need to show that the following relations hold:

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}\Delta^{op,\psi}(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\psi}(x)\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in H\ ,

(2.7)

\displaystyle(\Delta^{op,\psi}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}^{\psi\psi}\mathfrak{R}_{32}^{\psi\psi}\ ,

(2.8)

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op,\psi})(\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}^{\psi\psi}\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}\ .

Note that as corollaries of (
R1
)–(
R3
) we have:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\Delta(x)\mathfrak{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{21}\Delta^{op}(x)\ ,\qquad\forall x\in H\ ,

(2.10)

\displaystyle(\Delta^{op}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{32}\ ,

(2.11)

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{21}\ .

The relation (
2.6
) is obtained by applying
(\psi\otimes\psi)
to (
2.9
)

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes\psi)[\Delta(x)\mathfrak{R}_{21}]

\displaystyle=(\psi\otimes\psi)[\mathfrak{R}_{21}\Delta^{op}(x)]

\displaystyle[(\psi\otimes\psi)\Delta(\psi^{-1}\psi(x))]\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}[(\psi\otimes\psi)\Delta^{op}(\psi^{-1}\psi(x))]

\displaystyle\Delta^{\psi}(\psi(x))\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}\Delta^{op,\psi}(\psi(x))\ .

Next, rewrite (
2.10
) as

\displaystyle(\Delta^{op}\otimes{\text{id}})((\psi^{-1}\otimes\psi^{-1})\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{32}\ ,

\displaystyle(\Delta^{op}\circ\psi^{-1}\otimes\psi^{-1})(\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{32}\ ,

and apply
(\psi\otimes\psi\otimes\psi)
to get

\displaystyle(\Delta^{op,\psi}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})=\mathfrak{R}_{31}^{\psi\psi}\mathfrak{R}_{32}^{\psi\psi},

and (
2.11
) is obtained similarly.
∎

To introduce the concept of universal K-matrix, we also need another type of twists, called Drinfeld twists:

Definition 2.4

(
[
Dr86
,
Dr89a
]
)
.

A Drinfeld twist of a Hopf algebra H is an invertible element
J\in H\otimes H
satisfying the property

(2.12)

(\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})(J)=1=({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(J)

and the cocycle identity

(2.13)

(J\otimes 1)(\Delta\otimes{\text{id}})(J)=(1\otimes J)({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(J)\ .

Example 2.5
.

In the literature, there are two natural choices of Drinfeld twists:

J=1\otimes 1
and
J=\mathfrak{R}_{21}\mathfrak{R}
.
It is straightforward to check using the universal R-matrix axioms (
R1
)–(
R3
) that (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) indeed hold for both of them.

Given a Drinfeld twist
J
one obtains a new quasi-triangular Hopf algebra
(H_{J},\mathfrak{R}_{J})
with the coproduct
[
Dr89b
]

(2.14)

\Delta_{J}(x)=J\Delta(x)J^{-1}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in H\ ,

and the universal R-matrix

(2.15)

\mathfrak{R}_{J}=J_{21}\mathfrak{R}J^{-1}\ .

Definition 2.6

(
[
AV20
]
)
.

Let
(H,\mathfrak{R})
be a quasi-triangular algebra.
A twist pair
(\psi,J)
is the datum of an algebra automorphism
\psi\colon H\rightarrow H
and a Drinfeld twist
J\in H\otimes H
such that
(H^{cop,\psi},\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi})=(H_{J},\mathfrak{R}_{J})
, i.e. such that
\epsilon^{\psi}=\epsilon
,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\Delta^{op,\psi}(x)

\displaystyle=J\Delta(x)J^{-1}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in H\ ,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\psi\psi}

\displaystyle=J_{21}\mathfrak{R}J^{-1}\ ,

where

(2.17)

\Delta^{op,\psi}=(\psi\otimes\psi)\circ\Delta^{op}\circ\psi^{-1}.

2.3.
Universal K-matrix

Appel and Vlaar introduced the notion of a cylindrical bialgebra
[
AV20
, Def. 2.3]
which is a quasi-triangular bialgebra with a twist pair and a universal solution of a twisted reflection equation in
H\otimes H
. This approach is convenient for scalar K-matrix solutions of the parameter-dependent reflection equation (
1.2
). It is however not a convenient framework for K-operator solutions of more general reflection equations (
1.8
) associated to general
H
-comodule algebras
B
.
To change this, we define a universal K-matrix via axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
) and show that it satisfies a universal reflection equation in
B\otimes H\otimes H
that specializes on representations to the reflection equation (
1.8
), as will be further demonstrated in Section
4
. We begin with the following standard definition.

Definition 2.7
.

An algebra
B
is called a right comodule algebra over a Hopf algebra
H
if there exists an algebra map
\delta\colon B\rightarrow B\otimes H
, which we call right coaction, such that the coassociativity and counital conditions hold

(2.18)

({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)\circ\delta=(\delta\otimes id)\circ\delta\ ,\qquad({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)\circ\delta={\text{id}}\ .

Let
(\psi,J)
be a twist pair for a Hopf algebra
H
and
B
is a right comodule algebra over
H
.
We define a universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}\in B\otimes H
. Here we use the notation
\mathfrak{K}_{12}=\mathfrak{K}\otimes 1
,
\mathfrak{K}_{13}=\mathfrak{p}_{23}\circ\mathfrak{K}_{12}
.

Definition 2.8
.

We say that
\mathfrak{K}\in B\otimes H
is universal K-matrix if the following relations hold for all
b\in B
:

(K1)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\mathfrak{K}\delta(b)

\displaystyle=\delta^{\psi}(b)\mathfrak{K}\ ,\qquad\text{with }\delta^{\psi}=({\text{id}}\otimes\psi)\circ\delta\ ,

(K2)

\displaystyle(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ ,

(K3)

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{K})

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{12}\ ,

where

(2.19)

\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}=(\psi\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R})\ .

We note that
\delta^{\psi}
defines a coalgebra structure on
B
over
H^{\psi}
. Therefore, by (
K1
)
\mathfrak{K}
intertwines two actions of
B
on
B\otimes H
, given by
\delta
and
\delta^{\psi}
respectively. By analogy with (
R1
), we call (
K1
) the twisted intertwining relation.
We now make several remarks concerning this definition.

Remark 2.9
.

From the axioms (
K2
)–(
K3
) of the universal K-matrix, we get some relations on the level of the algebra.

(i)

We provide a consistency check of the axioms (
K2
) and (
K3
).
From coassociativity property (
2.18
), we have:

(2.20)

({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta\otimes{\text{id}})\circ(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})=(\delta\otimes{\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}})\circ(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})\ .

This relation is checked using (
R2
) and (
K2
). Indeed, the l.h.s. equals
(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{43}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{42}\mathfrak{K}_{14}\mathfrak{R}_{24}\mathfrak{R}_{34}
where we used
(\Delta\otimes{\text{id}})((\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21})=(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{31}
, while the r.h.s. gives the same expression using twice (
K2
).

The counital property in (
2.18
) is checked using (
K3
) and (
2.2
) as follows:

(2.21)

({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})\circ(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})=({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})((\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23})=\mathfrak{K}\ .

(ii)

Recall that the coproduct and the counit of a Hopf algebra satisfy

(2.22)

(\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})\circ\Delta={\text{id}}=({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta\ .

Then, using (
K3
), (
2.2
), (
2.12
), and the fact that
\epsilon
is an algebra homomorphism we obtain:

(2.23)

\mathfrak{K}=[({\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)\circ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)](\mathfrak{K})=({\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(J_{23}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{12})=([({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathfrak{K})]\otimes 1)\,\mathfrak{K}\ .

Applying again
({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)
on (
2.23
), we find that
({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathfrak{K})
is an idempotent.
If in addition,
\mathfrak{K}
is invertible, it follows:

(2.24)

({\text{id}}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathfrak{K})=1\ ,

which is the analogue of (
2.2
) for the universal R-matrix.

Remark 2.10
.

The previous definitions of universal K-matrices from the works
[
Ko17
,
AV20
]
can be obtained as special cases of Definition
2.8
.

(i)

It is easy to check that
({\text{id}},\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{-1})
is a twist pair. In particular, one finds that (
K1
)–(
K3
), for
\psi={\text{id}}
and
J=\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{-1}
, correspond to the axioms for the universal K-matrix defined in
[
Ko17
, Def. 2.7]
.

(ii)

Assume that
B
is a right coideal subalgebra of
H
, that is
\delta=\Delta|_{B}
, and define

{\bf\mathcal{K}}=(\epsilon\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})\ ,\qquad\text{${\bf\mathcal{K}}\in H$}\ .

Then, applying the counit on the first tensor factor of the universal K-matrix in (
K1
)–(
K3
) yields:

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\,b

\displaystyle=\psi(b)\mathcal{K}\ ,\qquad\forall b\in B\ ,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}\mathcal{K}_{2}\mathfrak{R}\ ,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\Delta|_{B}(\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{2}\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}\mathcal{K}_{1}\ .

We recall that in this setting of
B
a coideal subalgebra of
H
, Appel and Vlaar introduced in
[
AV20
]
one-component universal K-matrices
k\in H
.
The formulas (
2.25
) and (
2.27
), with the identification
\mathcal{K}=k
, correspond respectively to the defining relations of the so-called cylindrically invariant subalgebra
B
and the cylindrical pair
(H,\mathfrak{R})
, see
[
AV20
, Def. 2.3]
.
We thus get the one-component universal K-matrix of
[
AV20
]
from our 2-component
\mathfrak{K}
.
The opposite is also true: starting from a solution
\mathcal{K}
of (
2.25
) and (
2.27
), and assuming that
\mathfrak{K}
defined by (
2.26
) lies in
B\otimes H
, one can check that
\mathfrak{K}
satisfies the axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
).
Indeed, checking (
K1
) and (
K2
) is straightforward, while for (
K3
)
we use the equalities

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)[(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}]

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{31}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}J_{23}\ ,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}_{23}\mathfrak{R}_{13}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}_{23}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}\ .

The first one is obtained using

\Delta(\psi(x))=J^{-1}[(\psi\otimes\psi)\circ\Delta^{op}(x)]J\

with

({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})=\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{21}\ ,

while we applied
({\text{id}}\otimes\psi\otimes{\text{id}})\circ\mathfrak{p}_{12}
to the universal Yang-Baxter equation (
2.3
) to get the second equality.
Then, we get

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{K})

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}\;(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{31}\mathcal{K}_{3}\;(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}_{23}\mathfrak{R}_{13}\;\mathcal{K}_{2}\mathfrak{R}_{12}

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}\;(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{31}\mathcal{K}_{3}\;\mathfrak{R}_{13}\;\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}_{23}\;(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{21}\;\mathcal{K}_{2}\mathfrak{R}_{12}

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}^{\psi}_{23}\mathfrak{K}_{12}

which is indeed (
K3
).

Let us comment on the point (ii) of the above remark.
If
B=O_{q}
, the
q
-Onsager algebra, then an explicit formula for our universal K-matrix in
O_{q}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
could be deduced from (
2.26
) provided
\mathcal{K}
is known. Note that there are existence results for
\mathcal{K}
for certain choice of twists
[
AV20
]
.
However, no expressions for
\mathcal{K}
in
O_{q}
, for example in terms of root vectors, are known.
In the following, we make a choice of twist pair
(\psi,J)
that differs from
[
AV20
, Sec. 9.5]
. This allows to obtain the standard reflection equation from the
\psi
-twisted reflection equation (
2.28
) for our choice of principal gradation.
Let us also mention that in the next sections, we define K-operators out of
\mathfrak{K}\in\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, and derive fusion formulas satisfied by the K-operators for our choice of twist pair. As it will be studied in
[
LBG24
]
, these K-operators are involved in the construction of spin-chain transfer matrices.
In view of these applications, it is not clear whether the choice of the twist pair of Appel-Vlaar from
[
AV20
, Sec. 9.5]
produces the spin-chain transfer matrices.
Indeed, following the approach in (ii) of Remark
2.10
for
B=O_{q}
and using their twist pair, the fusion formula is affected by non-trivial
J
and it provides a priori different spin-
j
K-operators.

Furthermore, if
B
is a comodule algebra over
H
and not a coideal subalgebra of
H
, as in our main case of interest
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
, then the formalism from
[
AV20
,
AV22
]
is not suitable. That is why in this section we developed a slight generalization for the two-component universal K-matrices, and we study its consequences in the next sections.

We now derive a universal reflection equation based on the axioms (
K1
)–(
K3
) of the universal K-matrix.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
(\psi,J)
be a twist pair. The universal K-matrix satisfies the
\psi
-twisted reflection equation

(2.28)

\mathfrak{K}_{12}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}=\mathfrak{R}_{32}^{\psi\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{12}\ .

Proof.

Multiply (
K2
) on the left by
\mathfrak{K}_{12}
to get

(2.29)

\mathfrak{K}_{12}[(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})]=\mathfrak{K}_{12}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ .

Then, using (
K1
), the l.h.s. of this equation equals

(2.30)

[({\text{id}}\otimes\psi\otimes{\text{id}})\circ(\delta\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{K})]\mathfrak{K}_{12}=\mathfrak{R}_{32}^{\psi\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{12}\ ,

where we used again (
K2
).
Equating (
2.29
) with (
2.30
), the equation (
2.28
) follows.
∎

Note that the
\psi
-twisted reflection equation can also be derived from (
K3
). Multiply (
K3
) on the left by
\mathfrak{R}_{23}
then use (
R1
) to get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{23}[({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{K})]

\displaystyle=[({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op})(\mathfrak{K})]\mathfrak{R}_{23}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{23}J_{23}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\psi}\mathfrak{K}_{12}

\displaystyle=J_{32}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{12}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}\ .

Multiply (
2.31
) on the left by
J_{32}
. Since
(\psi,J)
is a twist pair, by (
2.15
) we have
\mathfrak{R}_{32}^{\psi\psi}=J_{32}\mathfrak{R}_{23}J_{23}^{-1}
and (
2.28
) follows.

2.4.
Example of
(H,B)

We now introduce our main example of the pair
(H,B)
where the Hopf algebra
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
is the quantum loop algebra and its right comodule algebra
B={\cal A}_{q}
is a central extension of the
q
-Onsager algebra.

2.4.1.
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}

We first recall the definition of the quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
corresponding to the presentation of Chevalley type. We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for definitions of the quantum algebra
U_{q}sl_{2}
and the quantum affine algebra
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
.

Definition 2.12
.

The quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
is the unital associative
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra generated by the elements
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}};\;i\in\{0,1\}
which satisfy the defining relations of the
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
algebra (
A.6
)-(
A.8
) with the extra relation

(2.32)

K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}=1=K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ .

The quantum loop algebra
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
is a Hopf algebra with the coproduct as in (
A.9
), counit in (
A.10
) and antipode (
A.11
) with the substitution
K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}=K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Note that a presentation of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
of Faddeev-Reshetikhin-Taktadjan type is also known
[
FRT87
]
. It involves Ding-Frenkel L-operators satisfying the Yang-Baxter algebra
[
DF93
]
, see details in Section
4.3
.

The expression of the universal R-matrix associated to quantum affine algebras is well known. Khoroshkin and Tolstoy first proposed the universal R-matrix associated with the quantized Kac–Moody algebras in
[
KT92a
, Thm. 2]
and then the arguments for its explicit form in terms of root vectors for
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
were provided in
[
KT92b
, eq. (58)]
6
6

6

As the universal R-matrix
\mathfrak{R}
of
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
has the form of a product over an
infinite
set of root vectors, and so strictly speaking
\mathfrak{R}
is not an element of the tensor product
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}\otimes U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
but belongs to its appropriate completion, see e.g.
[
AV22
, Sec. 2.5]
. This fact however does not affect the use of the R- or K-matrix axioms.
, see also
[
LSS93
,
Da98
]
.
In our work, we consider the universal R-matrix
\mathfrak{R}
associated with the Hopf algebra quotient
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and review its explicit form in our conventions in Appendix
B
. It is important to note that the coproduct convention we use is different to the one used in
[
KT92a
]
. The two coproducts and corresponding universal R-matrices are related via the automorphism
\nu
from (
A.12
):

(2.33)

\Delta^{\text{KT}}=(\nu\otimes\nu)\circ\Delta\circ\nu^{-1}\ ,\qquad(\nu^{-1}\otimes\nu^{-1})(\mathfrak{R}^{\text{KT}})=\mathfrak{R}\ ,

where
\mathfrak{R}^{\text{KT}}
denotes the image of the universal R-matrix given in
[
KT92b
, eq. (58)]
under the quotient map to
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, i.e. after setting the central charge to zero.

Let us introduce an automorphism
\eta
of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
:

(2.34)

\begin{matrix}\eta(E_{0})=F_{1}\ ,\qquad&\eta(E_{1})=F_{0}\ ,\qquad&\eta(K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})=K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\\
\eta(F_{1})=E_{0}\ ,\qquad&\eta(F_{0})=E_{1}\ ,\qquad&\eta(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})=K_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ .\end{matrix}

Example 2.13
.

For
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, both the pairs

(\psi,J)=(\eta,1\otimes 1)
and
(\psi,J)=(\eta,\mathfrak{R}_{21}\mathfrak{R})
, with the automorphism
\eta
from (
2.34
), are examples of twist pairs from Definition
2.6
. The first choice is the main example considered in this paper.

Let us prove the above statement for
(\psi,J)=(\eta,1\otimes 1)
, that is to verify (
2.16
). Using the definition of
\Delta
given in (
A.9
), we get
\Delta^{op,\eta}=\Delta
and therefore the first equation in (
2.16
) indeed holds. It remains to show that
\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\eta\eta}=\mathfrak{R}
. From Lemma
2.3
, the pair
(H^{cop,\eta},\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\eta\eta})
satisfies (
2.6
)–(
2.8
) with
\psi=\eta
. Recall that an invertible solution of (
R1
)–(
R3
) of the form
[
KT92b
, eq. (42)]
is unique
[
KT92b
, Theorem 7.1]
, and is given by
[
KT92b
, eq. (58)]
.
We then note that
\mathfrak{R}^{\eta\eta}_{21}
is of the same form
[
KT92b
, eq. (42)]
. Because we have
\Delta^{op,\eta}=\Delta
, then the equations (
2.6
)–(
2.8
) for
\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\eta\eta}
become simply (
R1
)–(
R3
) with the substitution
\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{21}^{\eta\eta}
. Therefore, as
\mathfrak{R}^{\eta\eta}_{21}
satisfies the same equations as
\mathfrak{R}
, it follows from the uniqueness that they are equal and
(\eta,1\otimes 1)
is thus a twist pair. Similarly, for the choice
J=\mathfrak{R}_{21}\mathfrak{R}
using
\Delta_{J}=\Delta
and
\mathfrak{R}_{J}=\mathfrak{R}
, recall (
2.14
) and (
2.15
),
one shows that
(\eta,\mathfrak{R}_{21}\mathfrak{R})
is also a twist pair.

2.4.2.
Evaluation map

In further sections, we will use the so-called evaluation map to study the tensor product representation of the algebra
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
.
Recall the algebra
U_{q}sl_{2}
defined in Appendix
A
. First, consider the algebra map

(2.35)

\varphi\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow U_{q}sl_{2}

defined by the equations:

(2.36)

\begin{matrix}\varphi(E_{0})&=&F\ ,\qquad\varphi(F_{0})&=&E\ ,\qquad\varphi(K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&K^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\\
\varphi(E_{1})&=&E\ ,\qquad\varphi(F_{1})&=&F\ ,\qquad\varphi(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&K^{\frac{1}{2}}\ .\end{matrix}

Let
\phi_{u}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-gradation automorphism of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}

(2.37)

\phi_{u}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\ ,

with
u\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, defined by

(2.38)

\begin{matrix}\phi_{u}(E_{0})&=&u^{-1}E_{0}\ ,\qquad\phi_{u}(F_{0})&=&uF_{0}\ ,\qquad\phi_{u}(K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\\
\phi_{u}(E_{1})&=&u^{-1}E_{1}\ ,\qquad\phi_{u}(F_{1})&=&uF_{1}\ ,\qquad\phi_{u}(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ .\end{matrix}

Then the evaluation map
\mathsf{ev}_{u}
7
7

7

For a more general evaluation map, see for instance
[
BGKNR12
, eq. (4.32)]
. In this paper we set
s_{0}=s_{1}=-1
.

(2.39)

\mathsf{ev}_{u}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow U_{q}sl_{2}

is defined by the composition

(2.40)

\mathsf{ev}_{u}=\varphi\circ\phi_{u}\ .

2.4.3.
Comodule algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}

Before giving our example of the comodule algebra
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
over
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, which is a central extension of the
q
-Onsager algebra, we recall the definition of the latter.
The
q
-Onsager algebra
O_{q}
definition
[
T99
,
B04
]
is given in terms of two generators
W_{0},W_{1}
satisfying the so-called
q
-Dolan-Grady relations:

(2.41)

\displaystyle[{W}_{0},[{W}_{0},[{W}_{0},{W}_{1}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]

\displaystyle=\rho[{W}_{0},{W}_{1}]\ ,

(2.42)

\displaystyle[{W}_{1},[{W}_{1},[{W}_{1},{W}_{0}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]

\displaystyle=\rho[{W}_{1},{W}_{0}]\ ,

where we fix

(2.43)

\rho=k_{+}k_{-}(q+q^{-1})^{2}\ ,\qquad\text{with}\,\ k_{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}.

The algebra
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
has first appeared in
[
BK05a
,
BS09
]
in the form of a reflection algebra that we review in Section
5
. It was understood later on that
\mathcal{A}_{q}
is isomorphic to the central extension of the
q
-Onsager algebra
[
BB17
,
T21a
]
, namely to
O_{q}\otimes\mathbb{C}[Z]
where
\mathbb{C}[Z]=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\dots]
is a polynomial algebra with infinitely many indeterminates
z_{i}
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Here, we recall a so-called
compact
presentation of this algebra.

Definition 2.14

(see
[
T21b
]
)
.

\mathcal{A}_{q}
is an associative algebra over
{\mathbb{C}}
generated by
{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}
,
{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}
,
\{{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
subject to the following defining relations:

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]

\displaystyle=\rho[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}]\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]

\displaystyle=\rho[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{G}}}_{1}]

\displaystyle=[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]_{q}]\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{G}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]

\displaystyle=[[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]_{q},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1}]

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1},[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k}]_{q}]_{q}]\ ,\qquad k\geq 1\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}[[[{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}]_{q},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]_{q},{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}]\ ,\qquad k\geq 1\ ,

\displaystyle[{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1},{{\mathsf{G}}}_{\ell+1}]

\displaystyle=0\ ,\qquad k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}\ ,

where
\rho
is given by (
2.43
).

We discuss the coaction map
\delta\colon{\cal A}_{q}\rightarrow{\cal A}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
using the reflection algebra presentation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
in Section
6.2
.
The corresponding coaction of the fundamental generators
{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\mathsf{W}}_{1}

takes the form:

(2.44)

\displaystyle\delta({{\mathsf{W}}}_{0})

\displaystyle=1\otimes\left(k_{+}q^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{1}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+k_{-}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{1}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}\otimes K_{1}\ ,

(2.45)

\displaystyle\delta({{\mathsf{W}}}_{1})

\displaystyle=1\otimes\left(k_{+}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{0}K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}+k_{-}q^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{0}K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}\otimes K_{0}\ .

In this paper, it will be sufficient to consider the evaluation of the coaction map
\delta
, denoted by
\delta_{w}
:

(2.46)

\delta_{w}=({\text{id}}\otimes{\mathsf{ev}}_{w})\circ\delta\colon\mathcal{A}_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes U_{q}sl_{2}\ .

The proof of the following proposition is postponed to the end of Section
5.5
.

Proposition 2.15
.

The evaluated coaction map
\delta_{w}\colon\mathcal{A}_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes U_{q}sl_{2}
is such that:

(2.47)

\displaystyle\delta_{w}({{\mathsf{W}}}_{0})

\displaystyle=1\otimes(k_{+}q^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{-1}EK^{\frac{1}{2}}+k_{-}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}wFK^{\frac{1}{2}})+{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0}\otimes K\ ,

(2.48)

\displaystyle\delta_{w}({{\mathsf{W}}}_{1})

\displaystyle=1\otimes(k_{+}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}wEK^{-\frac{1}{2}}+k_{-}q^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{-1}FK^{-\frac{1}{2}})+{{\mathsf{W}}}_{1}\otimes K^{-1}\ ,

(2.49)

\displaystyle\delta_{w}({{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{-}}{k_{+}}\frac{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}{q+q^{-1}}({{\mathsf{G}}}_{k}-\frac{(q+q^{-1})}{\rho}\big[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},\big[{{\mathsf{W}}}_{0},{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k}\big]_{q}\big])\otimes F^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(q-q^{-1})}{k_{+}(q+q^{-1})}\big(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}w^{-1}\big[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\mathsf{G}}_{k+1}\big]_{q}\otimes FK^{\frac{1}{2}}+q^{\frac{1}{2}}w\big[{\mathsf{G}}_{k+1},{\mathsf{W}}_{1}\big]_{q}\otimes FK^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

\displaystyle+{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k+1}\otimes 1-\frac{{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k}}{q+q^{-1}}\otimes(w^{2}K^{-1}\!+\!w^{-2}K)\ ,

with initial condition:

{{\mathsf{G}}}_{k}\bigr\rvert_{k=0}=k_{+}k_{-}\frac{(q+q^{-1})^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}\ .

3.
Tensor product representations of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and sub-representations

Our fusion procedure for K-operators requires first analysis of the tensor product of evaluation representations of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. The reducibility criteria in terms of ratios of the evaluation parameters for these tensor products are known
[
CP91
, Sect. 4.9]
. In this section,
we study the sub-quotient structure of these tensor products in more details, and construct explicitly the corresponding intertwining operators.
We do it first in Section
3.2
for finite-dimensional evaluation representations, and then in Section
3.5
for their infinite-dimensional analogues with formal evaluation parameters.
These operators are used in further sections to built fused L- and K-operators for any spin-
j
starting from the fundamental L- and K-operators.

First, recall that finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
U_{q}sl_{2}
are labeled by a non-negative integer or half-integer
j
, with the dimension of the representation being
2j+1
. Let
V^{(j)}
denotes the (
2j+1
)-dimensional space spanned by
\ket{j,m}
with
m\in\{-j
,
-j+1
,
\ldots
,
j-1
,
j\}
, then

(3.1)

E\ket{j,m}=A_{j,m}\ket{j,m+1},\qquad F\ket{j,m}=B_{j,m}\ket{j,m-1}\ ,\qquad K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\ket{j,m}=q^{\pm m}\ket{j,m}\ ,

with

(3.2)

A_{j,m}=\sqrt{\left[j-m\right]_{q}\left[j+m+1\right]_{q}}\ ,\qquad B_{j,m}=\sqrt{\left[j+m\right]_{q}\left[j-m+1\right]_{q}}\ .

Let
\pi^{j}
be the representation map of
U_{q}sl_{2}
:

(3.3)

\pi^{j}\colon U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})\ .

Now, given
u\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we then define the evaluation representations
\pi^{j}_{u}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})

by

(3.4)

\pi^{j}_{u}=\pi^{j}\circ\mathsf{ev}_{u}\ ,

where
\mathsf{ev}_{u}
is defined in (
2.40
), and the corresponding
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-module by
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{u}
.
We study now tensor products of these representations

(3.5)

(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\circ\Delta\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1})\ ,

and look at special points in the evaluation parameters space so that a proper sub-representation emerges.
The strategy is the following: we first construct basis vectors
\{w_{k}\},\{v_{\ell}\}
for the decomposition with respect to the subalgebra generated by
\{E_{1},F_{1},K_{1}\}
. Then, we study the action of
\{E_{0},F_{0},K_{0}\}
on these basis vectors. We find that there are only two ratios of evaluation parameters up to a sign when we get a proper sub-representation, and we also construct explicitly the corresponding intertwining maps.

3.1.
Analysis of the tensor product representation of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}

Consider first the subalgebra generated by
\{E_{1}
,
F_{1}
,
K_{1}\}
and construct basis vectors
\{w_{k}\},\{v_{\ell}\}
, where
k=0
,
1
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
,
\ell=0
,
1
,
\ldots
,
2j-1
and
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
, corresponding to the tensor product decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}=\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2j}
. We denote by
w_{0}
and
v_{0}
the highest weight vectors of the corresponding spins-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
and
(j-\frac{1}{2})
. These are defined by the relations

(3.6)

\displaystyle[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(E_{1})]w_{0}

\displaystyle=0\ ,\qquad[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(K_{1})]w_{0}=q^{2j+1}w_{0}\ ,

(3.7)

\displaystyle[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(E_{1})]v_{0}

\displaystyle=0\ ,\qquad\;[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(K_{1})]v_{0}=q^{2j-1}v_{0}\ .

Solutions to these equations are uniquely determined, up to a scalar, by

(3.8)

w_{0}=\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j}\ ,\qquad v_{0}=\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j-1}-\frac{u_{1}}{u_{2}}q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}A_{j,j-1}\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,j}\ ,

with
\ket{\uparrow}=\ket{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}},\ket{\downarrow}=\ket{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}}
, and where
A_{j,m}
is given in (
3.2
).
The other basis vectors are constructed via the action of
F_{1}
:

(3.9)

w_{k}=\left[(\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})\Delta(F_{1})\right]^{k}w_{0}\ ,\qquad v_{\ell}=\left[(\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})\Delta(F_{1})\right]^{\ell}v_{0}\ ,

where
0\leq k\leq 2j+1
and
0\leq\ell\leq 2j-1
, and we set these vectors to zero if their index is outside of the indicated range.

Now, we study the action of the generators
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
on these basis vectors, and we begin with the action on
w_{k}
’s:

(3.10)

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(E_{0})\right]w_{k}

\displaystyle=e_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{k+1}+e_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{k}\ ,

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(F_{0})\right]w_{k}

\displaystyle=f_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{k-1}+f_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{k-2}\ ,

where the coefficients are

(3.11)

\displaystyle e_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{1}^{-2}+[2j]_{q}u_{2}^{-2}\ ,

\displaystyle e_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2j+\frac{1}{2}}(u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2}q^{-2j-1})\sqrt{[2j]_{q}}}{u_{1}^{2}u_{2}\left[2j+1\right]_{q}}\ ,

(3.12)

\displaystyle f_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}[2j]_{q}\right)\frac{[2j-k+2]_{q}[k]_{q}}{[2j+1]_{q}}\ ,\;

\displaystyle f_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2}q^{-2j-1})\sqrt{[2j]_{q}}[k]_{q}[k-1]_{q}}{u_{2}^{-1}q^{-2j-\frac{1}{2}}[2j+1]_{q}}\ .

We next look at the action on
v_{\ell}
’s:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(E_{0})\right]v_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\bar{e}_{1,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{\ell+1}+\bar{e}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{\ell+2}\ ,

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(F_{0})\right]v_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\bar{f}_{1,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{\ell-1}+\bar{f}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{\ell}\ ,

where the coefficients are

(3.14)

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{1,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{2}^{-2}[2j+2]_{q}-u_{1}^{-2}}{[2j+1]_{q}}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2}q^{2j+1}}{q^{2j+\frac{1}{2}}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{3}\sqrt{[2j]_{q}}[2j+1]_{q}}\ ,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{1,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=(u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2}[2j+2]_{q})\frac{[\ell-2j]_{q}[\ell]_{q}}{[2j+1]_{q}}\ ,\quad

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2}q^{2j+1})[\ell-2j]_{q}[2j+1-\ell]_{q}}{u_{2}q^{2j+\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{[2j]_{q}}[2j+1]_{q}}\ .

We first note that in (
3.10
) and (
3.13
) the coefficients with indices
2,k
and
\textit{2,}\ell
, respectively, correspond to contribution in the action that mixes the two spin components. In order to find proper sub-representations, we thus need to analyze the roots of these functions
e_{2,k}
,
f_{2,k}
,
\bar{e}_{2,\ell}
and
\bar{f}_{2,\ell}
.
First, for generic values of
u_{1}/u_{2}
these functions do not vanish which corresponds to the well known fact that this tensor product is irreducible. For later convenience, we present this action diagrammatically below in Figure
1
where the red (resp. blue) arrows correspond to the action of
F_{0}
(resp.
E_{0}
), and the branching points correspond to the linear combinations from (
3.10
) and (
3.13
).

.

From the expression of the coefficients (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), one finds that

e_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})=f_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})=0\quad\text{iff}\quad u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}

and

\bar{e}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})=\bar{f}_{2,\ell}(u_{1},u_{2})=0\quad\text{iff}\quad u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}.

Bold arrows in Figure
1
are used to emphasize that they disappear after fixing the ratio
u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
. However we do not have simultaneously these four coefficients equal to zero so we do not have a direct sum decomposition. Instead, by fixing the ratio
u_{1}/u_{2}
to the special values we have a sub-representation as depicted below in Figure
2
by the dotted rectangles.

.

We thus find that fixing
u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
(resp.
u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
) gives a spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
(resp. spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
) sub-representation.

3.2.
Intertwining map
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and its pseudo-inverse
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

We now study the spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation when
u_{1}/u_{2}=q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
, and construct the corresponding intertwining operator explicitly.
Introduce two linear operators
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
as

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{u}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\ ,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{u}\ ,

given by:

(3.18)

\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\displaystyle{\sum_{a=1}^{4j+2}\sum_{b=1}^{2j+2}}\mathcal{E}_{a,b}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}E_{a,b}^{(2j,j)}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2j+2}\ ,

where
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{a,b}
are certain scalars,
E_{a,b}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}
denotes the matrix of dimension
(2j_{1}+2)\times(2j_{2}+2)
with 1 at position
(a,b)
and
0
otherwise. Here, we choose the bases of the source
\{\ket{j+\frac{1}{2},m}\}
with
m=j+\frac{1}{2}
,
j-\frac{1}{2}
,
\ldots
,
-j+\frac{1}{2}
,
-j-\frac{1}{2}
and the target
\{\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j}
,
\ldots
,
\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,-j}
,
\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,j}
,
\ldots
,
\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,-j}\}
.

We now calculate the coefficients
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{a,b}
from (
3.18
) provided
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner for the condition
u_{1}/u_{2}=q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
and
u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
, that was found in the previous subsection for
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
First of all for
j=0
, we have for any
u
that
\pi_{u}^{0}=\epsilon
, where the counit is defined in (
A.10
), i.e. the trivial representation of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. Then identifying
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, it follows
from (
3.16
), (
3.17
) and (
3.18
)
that
\mathcal{E}^{(\frac{1}{2})}=\mathcal{F}^{(\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
u_{1}/u_{2}=q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
and
u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then the map
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
in (
3.18
) is a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner

(3.19)

\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(x)=(\pi_{uq^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})(\Delta(x))\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\ ,

if and only if its entries are given for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
by

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=1\ ,\qquad\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{1+n,1+n}=\displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{n-1}}\frac{B_{j,j-p}}{B_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2}-p}}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{2j+1+m,1+m}=[m]_{q}\frac{\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{m,m}}{B_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{3}{2}-m}}\ ,

where
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j
,
m=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
and
B_{j,m}
is given in (
3.2
), and all the other entries are zero.

Proof.

Recall that for
u_{1}=u_{2}q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
we have a spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation as depicted in left-top of Figure
2
, and therefore we also have a corresponding intertwining operator that we are going to construct.
In this case, the sub-representation has the basis
\{w_{k}\,\rvert\,0\leq k\leq 2j+1\}
given in (
3.9
).
Let us introduce a basis
\{w^{\prime}_{k}\,\rvert\,0\leq k\leq 2j+1\}
in the source of the map
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
: we define
w_{0}^{\prime}=\ket{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2}}
and
w_{k}^{\prime}=[\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}}(F_{1})]^{k}w_{0}^{\prime}
. The intertwining property reads

(3.21)

\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\left[\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\right]w_{k}^{\prime}=\left[(\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})(\Delta(x))\right]\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(w_{k}^{\prime})\ ,\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\ .

This equation for
x=K_{0}
,
K_{1}
and
k=0
gives
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(w_{0}^{\prime})=w_{0}
. Then for
k=0
and
x=(F_{1})^{n}
, with
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
, one shows

(3.22)

\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(w_{n}^{\prime})=w_{n}\ ,\quad\text{for all $n$.}

Using this, we then check directly that (
3.21
) indeed holds now for all
k
and
x=F_{1}
and
x=E_{1}
. It remains to check that (
3.21
) is satisfied for the action of
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
. By straightforward calculations using (
2.38
), (
3.4
) and (
A.9
), one gets:

(3.23)

\displaystyle\left[\pi^{j+\frac{1}{2}}_{u}(E_{0})\right]w_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=u^{-2}w_{k+1}^{\prime}\ ,

\displaystyle\left[\pi^{j+\frac{1}{2}}_{u}(F_{0})\right]w_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=u^{2}w_{k-1}^{\prime}\ ,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(E_{0})\right]w_{k}

\displaystyle=qu_{2}^{-2}w_{k+1}\ ,

\displaystyle\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\Delta(F_{0})\right]w_{k}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}u_{2}^{2}w_{k-1}\ .

Using (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), one finds that (
3.21
) holds for
u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Finally, we get the matrix elements of
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
. The basis vectors read explicitly:

\displaystyle w_{k}

\displaystyle=u^{k}\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}}B_{j,j-p}\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j-k}+[k]_{q}\displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{k-2}}B_{j,j-p}\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,j-k+1}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle w_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=u^{k}\ \displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}}B_{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2}-p}\ket{j+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2}-k}\ ,

and we set
\displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{n}}B_{j,j-p}=1
if
n
is negative.
Then solving (
3.22
) for
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
in (
3.18
), one gets (
3.20
) (recall that the basis used in (
3.18
) is
\ket{j+\frac{1}{2},m}
with
m=j+\frac{1}{2}
,
j-\frac{1}{2}
,
\ldots
,
-j+\frac{1}{2}
,
-j-\frac{1}{2}
, and not
w^{\prime}_{k}
).
∎

We now give an expression of
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
which is a pseudo-inverse of
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
. It takes the form:

(3.25)

\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\displaystyle{\sum_{a=1}^{2j+2}\sum_{b=1}^{4j+2}}\mathcal{F}_{a,b}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}E_{a,b}^{(j,2j)}\ ,

where
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{a,b}
are scalars. The solution of
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2j+2}
is not unique. For instance, we fix the entries of
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
for
n=2
,
3
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
as follows:

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1,1}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=1\ ,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n,n+2j}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{n+2j,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}}{(\mathcal{E}_{n,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})^{2}+(\mathcal{E}_{n+2j,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})^{2}}\ ,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2j+2,4j+2}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}_{4j+2,2j+2}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})^{-1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{1-\mathcal{F}_{n,n+2j}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}_{n+2j,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}}{\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{n,n}}\ ,

and all other entries are zero. This choice is important because it allows the factorization of the R-matrix as in Lemma
3.5
below.
We finally note that any pseudo-inverse of
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
, in particular the one given above, is not a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner because the sub-representation involved is not a direct summand, recall the structure in Figure
2
.

3.3.
The maps
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}

We now study the spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation when
u_{1}/u_{2}=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
.
Introduce the two maps
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
:

(3.28)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{2j}_{u}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\ ,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2j}_{u}\ ,

given by:

(3.30)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=\displaystyle{\sum_{a=1}^{4j+2}\sum_{b=1}^{2j}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{a,b}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}E^{(2j,j-1)}_{a,b},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2j}\ ,

where
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{a,b}
are certain scalars. The bases of the source and the target of
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
are respectively
\{\ket{j-\frac{1}{2},m}\}
with
m=j-\frac{1}{2}
,
j-\frac{3}{2}
,
\ldots
,
-j+\frac{3}{2}
,
-j+\frac{1}{2}
and
\{\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j},\ldots,\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,-j},\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,j},\ldots,\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,-j}\}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
u_{1}/u_{2}=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
and
u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then the map
