\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
in (
3.30
) is a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner

(3.31)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(\pi_{u}^{j-\frac{1}{2}})(x)=(\pi_{uq^{j+1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})(\Delta(x))\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\ ,\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\ ,

if and only if its entries are given for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
by

(3.32)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{2,1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=1\ ,\qquad\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{2+n,1+n}=\displaystyle{\prod_{p=0}^{n-1}}\frac{B_{j,j-p-1}}{B_{j-\frac{1}{2},j-\frac{1}{2}-p}}\ \ ,\qquad\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{2j+2+m,1+m}=\frac{[m-2j]_{q}}{B_{j,j-m}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{2+m,1+m}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

where
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j-1
,
m=0
,
1
,
\ldots
,
2j-1
and
B_{j,m}
is given in (
3.2
), and all the other entries are zero.

Proof.

Recall that for
u_{1}=u_{2}q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
we have a spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation as depicted in right-bottom of Figure
2
. We also have a corresponding intertwining operator
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
that we now construct.
In this case, the sub-representation has the basis
\{v_{\ell}\,\rvert\,0\leq\ell\leq 2j-1\}
given in (
3.9
). Let us introduce a basis
\{v^{\prime}_{k}\,\rvert\,0\leq k\leq 2j-1\}
in the source of the map
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
: we define
v_{0}^{\prime}=\ket{j-\frac{1}{2},j-\frac{1}{2}}
and
v_{k}^{\prime}=[\pi_{u}^{j-\frac{1}{2}}(F_{1})]^{k}v_{0}^{\prime}
. Analogously to the proof of Lemma
3.1
, one shows
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(v_{\ell}^{\prime})=v_{\ell}
. Then, one finds the constraint on the ratio
u/u_{2}
and the coefficients (
3.32
) are obtained by solving the latter equation for
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
in (
3.30
).
∎

We now give expression of
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
which is a pseudo-inverse of
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
. It takes the form:

(3.33)

\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=\displaystyle{\sum_{a=1}^{2j}}\sum_{b=1}^{4j+2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{a,b}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}E^{(j-1,2j)}_{a,b}\ ,

where
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{a,b}
are scalars.
The solution of
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2j}
is not unique. Similarly to the fusion case above, we fix the entries of
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
for
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j
this way:

(3.34)

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{n,n+2j+1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{n+2j+1,n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}}{(\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{n+1,n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})})^{2}+(\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{n+2j+1,n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})})^{2}}\ ,\qquad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{n,n+1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{1-\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{n,n+2j+1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{n+2j+1,n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}}{\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{n+1,n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}}\ ,

and all other entries are zero. We note that, similarly to the previous case,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is not an intertwiner.

In summary, imposing some conditions on the ratio of evaluation parameters as in Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, the tensor product representation of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
admits a non-trivial sub-representation either of spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
or of spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
. And we have constructed intertwining operators
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}
and
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2j}
, and their pseudo-inverses
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
, respectively.

In what follows, we will need action on tensor product using opposite coproduct. For this new action
8
8

8

Recall that for a bialgebra
H
, we can define another bialgebra
H^{cop}
with the coproduct
\Delta^{op}
. Therefore,
\Delta^{op}
also defines an action of the algebra
H
on the tensor product of
H
-modules.
, the corresponding intertwining operators appear at different evaluation parameters.

Remark 3.3
.

Consider the opposite coproduct
\Delta^{op}=\mathfrak{p}\circ\Delta
with the definition for
\Delta
in (
A.9
). Using now the action of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
on the tensor product given by
\Delta^{op}
, we repeat the sub-representation analysis from Section
3.1
using the corresponding basis
\{\tilde{w}_{k}\,\rvert\,0\leq k\leq 2j+1\}
and
\{\tilde{v}_{\ell}\,\rvert\,0\leq\ell\leq 2j-1\}
defined by

\tilde{w}_{k}=\left[(\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})\Delta^{op}(F_{1})\right]^{k}\tilde{w}_{0}\ ,\qquad\tilde{v}_{\ell}=\left[(\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})\Delta^{op}(F_{1})\right]^{\ell}\tilde{v}_{0}\ ,

with
\tilde{w}_{0}=w_{0}
in (
3.8
), and
\tilde{v}_{0}
is the solution of (
3.7
) with the substitution
\Delta\rightarrow\Delta^{op}

\tilde{v}_{0}=\ket{\uparrow}\otimes\ket{j,j-1}-\frac{u_{1}}{u_{2}}q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}A_{j,j-1}\ket{\downarrow}\otimes\ket{j,j}\ .

Then, we find that the conditions on the evaluations parameter are different. Indeed, we have for the spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation:

(3.35)

u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad\,u=u_{2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

and for the spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
sub-representation:

(3.36)

u_{1}/u_{2}=\pm q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad u=u_{2}q^{\frac{1}{2}}\ .

Nevertheless, it leads to the intertwining operator
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
(resp.
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
) with the same matrix elements as in (
3.20
) (resp. in (
3.32
)). Indeed, the matrix elements are invariant under the replacement of
q
by
q^{-1}
.
Then, the corresponding intertwining properties for the conditions (
3.35
), (
3.36
) and the choice of the positive sign read respectively for all
x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
:

(3.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(x)=(\pi_{uq^{j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})(\Delta^{op}(x))\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(\pi_{u}^{j-\frac{1}{2}})(x)=(\pi_{uq^{-j-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}})(\Delta^{op}(x))\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\ .

Remark 3.4
.

Let us mention that in the literature there are different conventions for the coproduct of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. For example, consider the coproduct in
[
BGKNR10
]

(3.39)

\Delta^{\text{KT}}(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes 1+K^{-1}_{i}\otimes E_{i}\ ,\qquad\Delta^{\text{KT}}(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes F_{i}\ ,

with the representation of
U_{q}sl_{2}

(3.40)

\displaystyle E\ket{j,m}

\displaystyle=\ket{j,m+1}\ ,

\displaystyle F\ket{j,m}

\displaystyle=\left[j+m\right]_{q}\left[j-m+1\right]_{q}\ket{j,m-1}\ ,

\displaystyle K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\ket{j,m}

\displaystyle=q^{\pm m}\ket{j,m}\ .

For the coproduct (
3.39
), the values of
u
,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
coincide with Lemma
3.1
. However, the expressions of
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
,
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
are different.

3.4.
Additional properties

We conclude this section with a few observations on relations between the intertwining operator
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
, its pseudo-inverse
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and the R-matrix.
In the literature the expression of the R-matrix
R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)\in\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
is known
[
KR83
,
DN02
]
. It reads:

(3.41)

\displaystyle R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)=\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-2}}c(uq^{j-\frac{1}{2}-k})\times

\displaystyle\left((q-q^{-1})\left(\sigma_{+}\otimes\pi^{j}(F)+\sigma_{-}\otimes\pi^{j}(E)\right)+uq^{\frac{1}{2}({\mathbb{I}_{4j+2}}+\sigma_{z}\otimes\pi^{j}(H))}-u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}({\mathbb{I}}_{4j+2}+\sigma_{z}\otimes\pi^{j}(H))}\right),

where
\pi^{j}(E)
,
\pi^{j}(F)
are given in (
3.1
),
\left(\pi^{j}(H)\right)_{mn}=2(j+1-n)\delta_{m,n}
, with
m
,
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
, and where we use the scalar function

(3.42)

c(u)=u-u^{-1}\ .

Note that this R-matrix satisfies the unitarity property

(3.43)

R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u^{-1})=\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-1}}c(uq^{j+\frac{1}{2}-k})c(u^{-1}q^{j+\frac{1}{2}-k})\right)\mathbb{I}_{4j+2}\ .

Let
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
be invertible diagonal matrices given by:

(3.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\text{Diag}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{2j+2}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\ ,

(3.45)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\text{Diag}(\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})},\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{2}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})},\ldots,\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{2j}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})})\ ,

where
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{m}
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{n}
are scalars.

Inspired by
[
BLN15
]
, the R-matrix (
3.41
) admits two special points for which its rank drops below its maximum. Then at these points, the R-matrix decomposes in terms of the intertwining operator
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and the operator
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
, defined above, as follows:

Lemma 3.5
.

The R-matrix (
3.41
) at the point
u=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
has rank
2j+2
and decomposes as:

(3.46)

R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{j+\frac{1}{2}})=\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

where
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed by Lemma
3.1
,
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is given in (
3.25
) with (
3.26
), (
3.27
) and the entries of
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
are

(3.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{2j+2}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-2}}c(q^{2j-k})\right)(q^{2j+1}-q^{-2j-1})\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-2}}c(q^{2j-k})\right)\frac{(q-q^{-1})B_{j,-j-1+n}}{\mathcal{E}_{n,n}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{F}_{n,n+2j}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}}\ ,

for
n=2
,
3
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is given in Lemma
3.1
and its pseudo-inverse
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is not unique. However, imposing (
3.46
), with
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
defined in (
3.44
), fixes both
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
and
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
uniquely as we now show.
Indeed, solving
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\mathbb{I}}_{2j+2}
imposes (
3.27
) and
\mathcal{F}_{1,1}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=1
. There are still
2j
unfixed coefficients
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{n,n+2j}
, with
n=2
,
3
,
\ldots
,
2j+1
. They are fixed as in (
3.26
), as well as the entries of
\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
, by solving (
3.46
).
∎

Then, with the decomposition (
3.46
) and using the pseudo-inverse property
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\mathbb{I}_{2j+2}
, we have:

Corollary 3.6
.

The following relations hold:

(3.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{j+\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

(3.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{j+\frac{1}{2}})\ ,

(3.50)

\displaystyle R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{j+\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{j+\frac{1}{2}})\ .

We note that Lemma
3.5
and Corollary
3.6
will be used many times in Sections
5
and
6
, in particular to prove the reflection equation in Theorem
5.7
.

Similarly to Lemma
3.5
, for the second special point we have:

Lemma 3.7
.

The R-matrix (
3.41
) at the point
u=q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}}
has rank
2j
and is decomposed as:

(3.51)

R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(q^{-j-\frac{1}{2}})=\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

where
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed by Lemma
3.2
,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is given in (
3.33
) with (
3.34
) and
the entries of
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
are

(3.52)

\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}=\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-2}}c(q^{-k-1})\right)\frac{(q-q^{-1})B_{j,-j+n}}{\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{n+2j+1,n}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{n,n+1}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}}\ ,

for
n=1
,
2
,
\ldots
,
2j
.

3.5.
Formal evaluation representations

In the next sections, when we discuss L- and K-operators, and in calculations of 1-component evaluation of the universal R-matrix, we actually work with formal parameter
u
, not a complex number, because the evaluated expressions would not be well-defined if
u
was a number as in the evaluation representations considered above. Actually, in the evaluation of the universal R-matrix, such considerations were already accounted for in
[
Dr86
, Sec. 13]
and
[
FR92
, Sec. 4]
, see
[
He17
, Sec. 1]
for a review. For an evaluation of the universal K-matrix, similar precautions should be taken. One way to proceed is to introduce a formal-parameter analogue of the finite-dimensional evaluation representations from (
3.4
) and to establish the intertwining properties analogous to Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
on this formal level. In the literature, these representations of quantum affine algebras are also known
[
CG03
]
under the name
quantum loop modules
.

From now on, we assume that
u^{\pm 1}
is formal, and keeping for brevity the same notations, we define the formal evaluation representations
\pi^{j}_{u}
as

(3.53)

\pi^{j}_{u}=\pi^{j}\circ\mathsf{ev}_{u}\colon\;\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})[u^{\pm 1}]\ .

where the formal evaluation map

(3.54)

\mathsf{ev}_{u}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow U_{q}sl_{2}[u^{\pm 1}]\ ,

is defined by same formulas as in (
2.39
) but with
u^{\pm 1}
formal variables. We thus have explicitly on generators:

(3.55)

\begin{matrix}\pi^{j}_{u}(E_{0})&=&u^{-1}\pi^{j}(F)\ ,\qquad\pi^{j}_{u}(F_{0})&=&u\pi^{j}(E)\ ,\qquad\pi^{j}_{u}(K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&\pi^{j}(K^{-\frac{1}{2}})\ ,\\
\pi^{j}_{u}(E_{1})&=&u^{-1}\pi^{j}(E)\ ,\qquad\pi^{j}_{u}(F_{1})&=&u\pi^{j}(F)\ ,\qquad\pi^{j}_{u}(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})&=&\pi^{j}(K^{\frac{1}{2}})\ .\end{matrix}

In the case
u
is a complex number, this action is also known as the principal gradation
[
BDGZ93
]
. In our formal
u
case, we will also call it the action of
principal gradation
.

The map (
3.53
) and formulas (
3.55
) define the action of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
on the space
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]
of Laurent polynomials in
u
with coefficients being vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
. For example, the action of
E_{0}
is given by

\pi^{j}_{u}(E_{0})(xu^{n})=\pi^{j}(F)(x)u^{n-1}\ ,\qquad x\in\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}\ ,\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\ ,

and extended linearly to all elements in
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]
, and similarly for the other generators.
Here, we use the same notation for the corresponding representation
\pi^{j}_{u}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\to\text{\rm End}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]\bigr)
, and call it
formal evaluation representation
.
We denote the corresponding infinite-dimensional module by

\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{u}
and sometimes call it also
quantum loop module of spin-
j
. In what follows, we will often use the notation
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{zu}
, for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, which means that the
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
action is on the same underlying vector space
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]
but one needs to replace
u
in the action (
3.55
) by
zu
.

Let
\{x_{k}\}_{0\leq k\leq 2j}
be a basis in the spin-
j
module
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
over
U_{q}sl_{2}
such that
x_{0}
is the highest weight vector, or
\ket{j,j}
in the notations of (
3.1
), and
x_{k}:=\pi^{j}(F)^{k}x_{0}
. Then the vectors

(3.56)

x_{k,n}:=x_{k}u^{n}\ ,\qquad 0\leq k\leq 2j\ ,\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\ ,

form a basis in
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{u}
with the action of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
up to scalars described as follows:
E_{0}
changes the index
(k,n)
by
(+1,-1)
, while
F_{0}
changes it in the opposite way
(k,n)\to(k-1,n+1)
, and similarly
E_{1}
changes the index
(k,n)
by
(-1,-1)
while
F_{1}
changes it by
(+1,+1)
, and finally
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
don’t change the index
(k,n)
, they act as
K^{-1}
and
K
, respectively, on
x_{k}
, i.e. by the corresponding
U_{q}sl_{2}
weight.

For the
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
action in the basis (
3.56
), let us consider an example of the
j=1
case in more details. The action of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
is better to present diagrammatically as in Figure
3
where each node corresponds to some
x_{k,n}
while edges are the actions of
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
described up to scalars above.

x_{\tiny{2,2}}

x_{\tiny{1,2}}

x_{\tiny{0,2}}

x_{\tiny{2,1}}

x_{\tiny{1,1}}

x_{\tiny{0,1}}

x_{\tiny{2,0}}

x_{\tiny{1,0}}

x_{\tiny{0,0}}

x_{\tiny{2,-1}}

x_{\tiny{1,-1}}

x_{\tiny{0,-1}}

x_{\tiny{2,-2}}

x_{\tiny{1,-2}}

x_{\tiny{0,-2}}

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

Figure 3.
Quantum loop module
\mathbb{C}^{3}_{u}
of spin-1 where the red and blue diagrams correspond to irreducible components of the
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
action.

As we can see from this example, the formal-evaluation representation
\mathbb{C}^{3}_{u}
is fully reducible due to the presence of two different sub-diagrams, one is blue and the other is red. Each of these sub-diagrams describes an irreducible action. It is not hard to see that this action arises from the other version of formal-evaluation representation in the homogeneous gradation.
Recall
[
BDGZ93
]
that the homogeneous gradation action is as in (
3.55
) for
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
, while
E_{1}
and
F_{1}
act just as
E
and
F
, respectively. Let us denote the corresponding
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-module on the vector space
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]
by
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2j+1}_{u}
and call it
quantum loop module of spin-
j
in the homogeneous gradation
. It is clear from the action in the basis of
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2j+1}_{u}
analogous to
x_{k}u^{n}
that this module is irreducible, while in our example
\mathbb{C}^{3}_{u}
is decomposed as
\mathbb{C}^{3}_{u}\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{3}_{u}\oplus\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{3}_{u}
.

We have a similar result for arbitrary spin
j
: indeed, the diagram for
j=\frac{1}{2}
is obtained by simply deleting the last row of nodes labeled by
x_{2,n}
and all arrows attached to them, so we get a disjoint union of two zig-zag’s, while for
j>1
one just repeats the obvious pattern of arrows which gives again two independent sub-diagrams of different color. Let us reformulate this observation as the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{u}
is a direct sum of two copies of
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2j+1}_{u}
.

This effect of decomposition of the action in one gradation via actions in the other gradation is in contrast to the finite-dimensional evaluation representations where the choice of gradation is not really important.

We now want to study tensor products of these infinite-dimensional representations

(3.57)

(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\circ\Delta\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1})\ ,

where we used that the homomorphism
(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\circ\Delta
sends
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
to
\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2})\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1})
which is a subalgebra in
\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1})
, and this algebra embedding is implicitly used in (
3.57
).

We then note that as a vector space
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},u_{2}^{\pm 1}]
– the space of Laurent polynomials in two variables
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
with coefficients in the
2(2j+1)
-dimensional vector space – or equivalently, to the product of
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
and
\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},u_{2}^{\pm 1}]
. Below, we will use these identifications implicitly.
Then, the action (
3.57
) can be written as

(3.58)

a\bigl(\boldsymbol{v}f(u_{1},u_{2})\bigr)=a(\boldsymbol{v})f(u_{1},u_{2})=\left[(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})\circ\Delta(a)\right]\!(\boldsymbol{v})f(u_{1},u_{2})

for any
a\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
and
f(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},u_{2}^{\pm 1}]
. In what follows, we will shortly write the action as on the left-hand side instead of lengthy but more precise expression on the right-hand side.

The difference from the finite-dimensional case where
u_{i}
’s were generic complex numbers is that these infinite-dimensional tensor products are not irreducible anymore. Indeed, for every
non-zero
complex number
z
the Laurent polynomial

u_{1}-z^{-1}u_{2}

generates a proper ideal in the algebra of Laurent polynomials because its inverse lives in a bigger ring of rational functions in
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.
Similarly, for the module
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
consider for a fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
the linear span of vectors of the following form:

(3.59)

I_{z}:=\langle\;\boldsymbol{v}\,(u_{1}-z^{-1}u_{2})f(u_{1},u_{2})\ |\;\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}\,,\,f(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},u_{2}^{\pm 1}]\;\rangle\ ,

which can be thought of as ‘ideal’ generated by the Laurent polynomials
u_{1}-z^{-1}u_{2}
.
Analyzing the action of
F_{i}
and
E_{i}
on these vectors,
recall (
3.58
), it is clear that
I_{z}
is a proper
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-submodule of
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
.

We can then define a so-called partial specialization “identifying
u_{2}
with
zu_{1}
” of the module
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
as the quotient by the submodule
I_{z}
from (
3.59
). We will use the following short-hand notations for this quotient:

(3.60)

\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{zu_{1}}^{2j+1}:=\Bigl(\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\Bigr)/I_{z}\ ,

when the same formal parameter appears in both tensor factors.
And let us denote the canonical surjection

(3.61)

p^{j}_{z}:\;\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{zu_{1}}^{2j+1}\ ,

which is a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwining operator. We also note that the quotient module
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{zu_{1}}^{2j+1}
can be identified with
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u_{1}^{\pm 1}]
as a vector space. Indeed, first note that the images
p^{j}_{z}(x\otimes y\,u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m})
, for any
x\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
y\in\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
are all proportional to
(x\otimes y)u_{1}^{n+m}
, therefore every element in the quotient space
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{zu_{1}}^{2j+1}
is a (finite) linear combination of the monomials
(x\otimes y)u_{1}^{n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which makes it identical to
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u_{1}^{\pm 1}]
. With this identification, the
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
representation corresponding to the quotient module (
3.60
) will be denoted by

(3.62)

\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{zu_{1}}^{j}\;\colon\;\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\to\text{\rm End}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]\bigr)\ .

Also, we will often use the notation
\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{zu_{1}}^{j}(\Delta(x))
, for
x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, to stress that the action in (
3.62
) uses the coproduct by construction. In other words,
\pi_{u_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{zu_{1}}^{j}(\Delta(x))
stands for the action of
\Delta(x)
on
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
reduced to the quotient space by the surjective map
p^{j}_{z}
from (
3.62
). We note that the intertwining property of
p^{j}_{z}
guarantees that this reduction is well defined, because the kernel of
p^{j}_{z}
is invariant under the action of
\Delta(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
.

In the quotient modules (
3.60
), we can now identify for appropriate values of
z
infinite-dimensional submodules isomorphic to certain quantum loop modules. First, denote by
W^{j}
the subspace in
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
spanned by
vectors
w_{k,n,m}
obtained via the iterated action of
F_{1}
on
w_{0}u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}
where
w_{0}
is the product (
3.8
) of highest-weight vectors in
U_{q}sl_{2}
-modules of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
and
j
:

(3.63)

w_{k,n,m}:=F_{1}^{k}(w_{0})\,u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}\ ,\qquad 0\leq k\leq 2j+1\ ,\;n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\ .

Note that as in the finite-dimensional situation if
k>2j+1
then the corresponding vectors
w_{k,n,m}
are just zero. We will also denote
w_{k}:=w_{k,0,0}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

The image of
W^{j}=\langle\,w_{k,n,m}\,\rangle_{0\leq k\leq 2j+1,n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
under the projection
p^{j}_{z}
from (
3.61
) at
z=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
is a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-submodule isomorphic to the quantum loop module
\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{u_{1}q^{j}}
of spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

First, the action of
E_{1}
preserves
W^{j}
because using the commutator relation between
E_{1}
and
F_{1}
and the fact that all vectors
w_{0,n,m}
are annihilated by
E_{1}
we get

E_{1}(w_{k,n,m})\sim w_{k-1,n,m}\ ,

and so the image of
W^{j}
under
p^{j}_{z}
is equally preserved by the action of
E_{1}
because
p^{j}_{z}
commutes with all
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
.
Let us now analyze the action of
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
on the vectors
w_{k,n,m}
. Observing that
w_{k,n,m}=w_{k}u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}
, we get for the action the expressions similar to (
3.10
):

(3.64)

\displaystyle E_{0}(w_{k,n,m})

\displaystyle=E_{0}(w_{k})u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}=e_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{k+1,n,m}+e_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{k}u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}\ ,

\displaystyle F_{0}(w_{k,n,m})

\displaystyle=F_{0}(w_{k})u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}=f_{1,k}(u_{1},u_{2})w_{k-1,n,m}+f_{2,k}(u_{1},u_{2})v_{k-2}u_{1}^{n}u_{2}^{m}\ ,

where
v_{k}
is defined as in (
3.9
) and the coefficients are as in (
3.11
)-(
3.12
).
Note that the images
p^{j}_{z}(w_{k,n,m})
are all proportional to
p^{j}_{z}(w_{k})u_{1}^{n+m}
, so the vectors
w_{k,n}:=p^{j}_{z}(w_{k})u_{1}^{n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
0\leq k\leq 2j+1
, form a basis in the image of
W^{j}
under the projection
p^{j}_{z}
.
Then, the action on these basis elements
w_{k,n}
at
z=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}
simplifies to:

(3.65)

\displaystyle E_{0}(w_{k,n})

\displaystyle=q^{-2j}[2j+1]_{q}\,w_{k+1,n-2}\ ,

\displaystyle F_{0}(w_{k,n})

\displaystyle=q^{2j}[2j+2-k]_{q}\,w_{k-1,n+2}\ .

We thus see that
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
acts on
p^{j}_{z}(W^{j})
. We are just left to identify the quantum loop module
p^{j}_{z}(W^{j})
is isomorphic to.
First note from (
3.65
) that the action of
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
changes the index
n
by
\pm 2
while the action of
E_{1}
and
F_{1}
keeps it unchanged. Therefore
p^{j}_{z}(W^{j})
is decomposed onto a direct sum of two
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-modules: one generated by
w_{0,0}
and the other by
w_{0,1}
.
We identify each of these modules with the quantum loop module
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{2j+2}_{u_{1}q^{j}}
in the homogeneous gradation, using essentially the same analysis as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
. Then using Lemma
3.8
we conclude that the image
p^{j}_{z}(W^{j})
is isomorphic to the quantum loop module
{\mathbb{C}}^{2j+2}_{u_{1}q^{j}}
which proves the proposition.
∎

We can now construct an intertwining operator corresponding to the quantum loop submodule described in Proposition
3.9
:

(3.66)

\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{uq^{j}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}_{u}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{uq^{j+1/2}}

where for simplicity we use the same notation as in the finite-dimensional setting of Section
3.2
.
First, we identify the quotient space
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{u}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{uq^{j+1/2}}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}[u^{\pm 1}]
as discussed below (
3.61
). Using this identification, we define the action of
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
on Laurent polynomials from
\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{uq^{j}}=\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}[u^{\pm 1}]

simply on their coefficients by the same matrix as in (
3.18
), with the matrix entries from Lemma
3.1
given in the basis fixed below (
3.18
). This action on coefficients is well defined because the matrix entries do not depend on
u
. We then extend this action linearly to Laurent polynomials in
u
.
With the discussion below (
3.62
),
it is straightforward to check the intertwining property (
3.19
) of such linear map
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
along the same lines as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
Similarly, its pseudo inverse map
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is first defined on coefficients of Laurent polynomials as in (
3.25
) with the same matrix entries (
3.26
)-(
3.27
), and then extended by linearity. Note that
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is not a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner.

Finally, we have a construction of intertwining map
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
analogous to Section
3.3
. This map corresponds to the spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
quantum loop submodule in the quotient module
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{u}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{uq^{-j-1/2}}
.
To see this, one can follow the proof of Proposition
3.9
but using the vector
v_{0}
from (
3.8
) instead of
w_{0}
. We thus get a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner

(3.67)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2j}_{uq^{-j-1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}_{u}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}_{uq^{-j-1/2}}

defined on coefficients of Laurent polynomials by the matrix (
3.30
), with the matrix entries from Lemma
3.2
. This map satisfies the same relation (
3.31
), now in the context of discussion below (
3.62
).
Similarly, its pseudo inverse map
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is defined on coefficients of Laurent polynomials via (
3.33
) and (
3.34
).

4.
Spin-
j
L- and K-operators

In sections
4.1
and
4.2
, we define spin-
j
L- and K-operators, respectively, as evaluations of universal R- and K-matrices for
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and
B
a comodule algebra for a certain twist pair. Using the intertwining operators studied in the previous section,
we show that the spin-
j
L- and K-operators satisfy certain properties named as ‘fusion’ and ‘reduction’. We also establish the so-called P-symmetry and unitarity properties of the fused L-operators. In the final subsection
4.3
, the comodule algebra structure on
B
is characterized within the framework of the spin-
j
K-operators and the Ding-Frenkel L-operators.

4.1.
Spin-
j
L-operators

4.1.1.
Evaluation of the universal R-matrix.

As we noted before,
the universal R-matrix
\mathfrak{R}
for
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
is an element of a certain completion of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
(see details e.g. in
[
AV22
, Sec. 2.5]
), expressed as a product over an infinite set of root vectors
[
KT92a
, Thm. 1]
, see also
[
KT92b
,
LSS93
,
Da98
]
. We give the universal R-matrix expression in our conventions in Appendix
B
. In this section, we consider 1- and 2-component evaluation of
\mathfrak{R}
on the formal evaluation representations

\pi^{j}_{u}
studied in the previous section
3.5
, recall (
3.53
) and (
3.55
).

Definition 4.1
.

For
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
, we define

(4.1)

{\bf L}^{(j)}(u_{1}/u_{2})=(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})(\mathfrak{R})\quad\in\;U_{q}sl_{2}[[u_{2}/u_{1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})\ .

We call
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)
the spin-
j
L-operator. In particular,
{\bf L}^{(0)}(u)=1
by (
2.2
).

A few comments for the above definition are necessary:

•

Strictly speaking, the right hand side of (
4.1
) does not make sense literally because
\mathfrak{R}
does not belong to
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
but to its completion which is much bigger and
a priori
is not of a tensor product form, so we cannot apply a map of this form. What makes sense is to use a bi-gradation on the positive part
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}^{+}
generated by
E_{i}
: the degree of
E_{0}
is
(1,0)
, while
E_{1}
has degree
(0,1)
. In other words, replacing each
E_{i}
by
z_{i}E_{i}
in the
q
-exponent expression (
B.4
) of
\mathfrak{R}
produces
\mathfrak{R}(z_{0},z_{1})

such that
q^{-\frac{1}{2}h_{1}\otimes h_{1}}\mathfrak{R}(z_{0},z_{1})\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}^{-}[[z_{0},z_{1}]]
. Now, we can apply a map of tensor product form coefficient-wise, and set
z_{0}=z_{1}=1
in the end of the calculation. This is what we will always use implicitly in the exposition below.

•

First, we evaluate 2nd tensor component of
\mathfrak{R}
at
j=\frac{1}{2}
treating
u_{2}
as a formal variable. This is done in Appendix
B.3.2
using the exponential form of
\mathfrak{R}
reviewed in Appendix
B.2
. This calculation essentially amounts to evaluating infinite series of the root vectors with (
3.55
), that is why one needs to consider
u_{2}
as a formal variable and not just a complex number. The result of such evaluation is the L-operator of type
\widehat{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u_{2})
from (
1.5
) for
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, and it takes the form of the Ding-Frenkel L-operator
[
DF93
]
. It is a
2\times 2
matrix with entries from
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}[[u_{2}]]
.

•

In the second step, it is straightforward to evaluate the remaining 1st component of
\mathfrak{R}
with (
3.54
), see our calculation in Appendix
B.4
, and with the final result that depends on the ratio
u=u_{1}/u_{2}
only:

(4.2)

{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mu(u){\bf{\cal L}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\ ,\;\mbox{with}\quad{\bf{\cal L}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}uq^{\frac{1}{2}}K^{\frac{1}{2}}-u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}K^{-\frac{1}{2}}&(q-q^{-1})F\\
(q-q^{-1})E&uq^{\frac{1}{2}}K^{-\frac{1}{2}}-u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}K^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix},

where the ‘normalization’
\mu(u)
is the following formal power series in
u^{-1}
:

(4.3)

\mu(u)=u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{\Lambda(u^{-2}q^{-1})}\ ,

and
\Lambda(u)
is a formal power series in
u
with coefficients
from the center
Z(U_{q}sl_{2})
, see its explicit expression in (
B.34
).
Formally, the result of evaluation in (
4.1
) belongs to
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u_{1}^{-1},u_{2}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, however we see that it actually belongs to a smaller space which is
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u_{2}/u_{1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
Furthermore, we show by the fusion construction discussed below that all the L-operators
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u_{1}/u_{2})
are indeed formal power series belonging to
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
with
u=u_{1}/u_{2}
, as indicated in Definition
4.1
.

Remark 4.2
.

The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)

was initially calculated
in
[
BGKNR12
, eq. (4.62)]
9
9

9

With the identification
\zeta\rightarrow u
,
s\rightarrow-2
, in particular
e^{\Lambda(q^{-1}\zeta^{s})}\rightarrow\mu(u)uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and
s_{0}\rightarrow-1
,
s_{1}\rightarrow-1
.
, with different conventions on the coproduct.
The two L-operators are related as follows. Recall that the automorphism (
2.33
) relates the universal R-matrix corresponding to our coproduct with the universal R-matrix of
[
KT92a
]
, the one used by
[
BGKNR12
]
. Then (
4.1
) for
j=\frac{1}{2}
reads

(4.4)

{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})=({\text{id}}\otimes\pi^{\frac{1}{2}})\circ(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\mathsf{ev}_{u_{2}})\circ(\nu^{-1}\otimes\nu^{-1})(\mathfrak{R}^{KT})\ ,

where
\nu
is given in (
A.12
).
Now, consider the automorphism
\tau\colon U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow U_{q}sl_{2}
defined by

(4.5)

\tau(E)=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}EK^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad\tau(F)=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}FK^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad\tau(K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\ .

Noting that it satisfies the property
\mathsf{ev}_{u}\circ\nu^{-1}(x)=\tau\circ\mathsf{ev}_{uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}}(x)
, for all
x\in\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, and
\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}\circ\tau=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
then (
4.4
) becomes

(4.6)

{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})=(\tau\otimes\pi^{\frac{1}{2}})\circ(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\otimes\mathsf{ev}_{u_{2}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}})(\mathfrak{R}^{KT})=(\tau\otimes{\text{id}})\bigl({\bf L}^{(KT)}(u_{1}/u_{2})\bigr)\ ,

where
{\bf L}^{(KT)}(u)
is the L-operator from
[
BGKNR12
, eq. (4.62)]
.

Evaluating the first component of
{\bf L}^{(j_{2})}(u)
on the spin-
j_{1}
representation of
U_{q}sl_{2}
we get the R-matrix for any spin
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})

\displaystyle=(\pi^{j_{1}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j_{2}}_{u_{2}})(\mathfrak{R})

\displaystyle=(\pi^{j_{1}}\otimes{\text{id}})({\bf L}^{(j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2}))\ ,

which
is a matrix from
\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j_{1}+1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j_{2}+1})
with entries in formal power series
\mathbb{C}[[u^{-1}]]
.

Example 4.3
.

For
j_{1}=j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
in (
4.7
), the corresponding R-matrix is given by:

(4.8)

\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu(u))R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}c(uq)&0&0&0\\
0&c(u)&c(q)&0\\
0&c(q)&c(u)&0\\
0&0&0&c(uq)\end{pmatrix}\ ,

where
c(u)
is given in (
3.42
) and with

(4.9)

\pi^{j}(\mu(u))=u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{q^{k(2j+1)}+q^{-k(2j+1)}}{1+q^{2k}}\frac{u^{-2k}}{k}\right)\ ,

where we used the evaluation of the coefficients
C_{k}
of
\Lambda(u)
given in (
B.34
), see
[
BGKNR12
, eq. (4.59)]
.
Note that
R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)
coincides with the expression in (
3.41
) for
j=\frac{1}{2}
.

We recall that the L-operators satisfy the RLL relations.
Indeed, applying
(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j_{2}}_{u_{2}}\otimes\pi^{j_{3}}_{u_{3}})
to (
2.3
), one finds
10
10

10

The RLL equation belongs to the triple product
U_{q}sl_{2}\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j_{1}+1})\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j_{2}+1})
, and thus the L-operator should be written as
{\bf L}^{(j)}_{0i}(u)
but here we omit the label
0
corresponding to
U_{q}sl_{2}
.

(4.10)

{\bf L}_{1}^{(j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2}){\bf L}_{2}^{(j_{3})}(u_{1}/u_{3})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2}/u_{3})=\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2}/u_{3}){\bf L}_{2}^{(j_{3})}(u_{1}/u_{3}){\bf L}_{1}^{(j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ .

Recall also that the R-matrix satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation.
It is found by applying
(\pi^{j_{1}}\otimes{\text{id}}\otimes{\text{id}})
to the above equation and setting
u_{3}=1
:

(4.11)

\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{3})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{23}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2})=\mathcal{R}_{23}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{3})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ .

We recall that the so-called quantum determinant is given by
[
Sk88
]

(4.12)

\displaystyle{\gamma}(u)

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}_{12}\big(\mathcal{P}_{12}^{-}{\cal L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\cal L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq)\big)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle=u^{2}q^{2}+u^{-2}q^{-2}-C\ ,

where
{\text{tr}}_{12}
stands for the trace over
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
, and
{\cal P}^{-}_{12}=(1-{\cal P})/2
with the permutation matrix
\cal P=R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(1)/(q-q^{-1})
, and the Casimir element
C
is defined in (
A.5
).

Remark 4.4
.

The inverse of
\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is given by

(4.13)

[\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)]^{-1}=-\frac{1}{\gamma(uq^{-1})}\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1})\ ,

with
\gamma(u)
from (
4.12
). Note that
u^{-2}\gamma(u)
is invertible in
Z(U_{q}sl_{2})[[u^{-1}]]
due to
[
T21d
, Lem. 4.1]
, and so
\gamma(u)
is invertible too.

4.1.2.
L-operators and fusion
(\frac{1}{2},j)\rightarrow(j+\frac{1}{2})
.

We study a so-called fusion relation for L-operators that expresses
{\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)
in terms of product of
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u_{1})
and
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u_{2})
. For this, we evaluate 2nd and 3rd tensor components of the universal R-matrix equation (
R3
) on quantum loop modules with formal evaluation parameters fixed as
u_{2}=q^{j+\frac{1}{2}}u_{1}
. This fixing requires taking the quotient (
3.60
) to relate the two formal variables
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
on tensor product (
3.57
) of two quantum loop modules
\mathbb{C}_{u_{1}}^{2}
and
\mathbb{C}_{u_{2}}^{2j+1}
, respectively.
Using Proposition
3.9
, on this quotient module
we get a
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-submodule isomorphic to the quantum loop module
\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}_{u_{1}q^{j}}
of spin-
(j+\frac{1}{2})
, with the corresponding intertwining operator
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
described below (
3.66
). As it was explained there, this intertwiner
is defined by Lemma
3.1
and its pseudo-inverse
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed by (
3.25
) with (
3.26
), (
3.27
).
Using the associated identity
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\text{id}}_{\mathbb{C}^{2j+2}[u^{\pm 1}]}
, fusion relations satisfied by L-operators and R-matrices are exhibited in the following two propositions.

Proposition 4.5
.

The spin-
j
L-operators (
4.1
) satisfy for
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
the following relations in
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+2})
:

(4.14)

{\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\bf L}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}){\bf L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

where we use the notation
{\langle 12\rangle}
to indicate which spaces are fused, i.e. where the intertwiner
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
acts, and here we use its finite-dimensional matrix form given in (
3.18
) with (
3.20
).

Proof.

By definition of the L-operator we have

(4.15)

{\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(w/u)=(\mathsf{ev}_{w}\otimes\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R})\ .

Using the pseudo-inverse property
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\text{id}}
, we get

\displaystyle{\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(w/u)

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\left[(\mathsf{ev}_{w}\otimes\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R})\right]

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\left[(\mathsf{ev}_{w}\otimes\pi_{uq^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{R})\right](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\left[(\mathsf{ev}_{w}\otimes\pi_{uq^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})(\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{12})\right](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}){\bf L}_{2}^{(j)}(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}w/u){\bf L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(q^{j}w/u)(1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\ ,

where in the second line we use the convention discussed below (
3.62
).
Here, the second equality is obtained using the intertwining property (
3.19
) which is applicable in the formal
u
setting too, following the discussion below (
3.66
). Applied to elements of 2nd tensor component of
\mathfrak{R}
this intertwining property takes the following form:

(4.16)

(1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\left[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R})\right]=\left[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})\circ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{R})\right](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\ ,

where the maps of the tensor product form are applied to
\mathfrak{R}(z_{0},z_{1})
coefficient-wise with respect to the bi-grading we always use to treat properly the universal R-matrix expansion, recall the first comment below Definition
4.1
.
Then, the third equality in the above calculation is due to (
R3
), and the last one is by definition of the L-operator.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

The R-matrices (
4.7
)
satisfy for all
j_{1},j_{2}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
the following relations in
\mathbb{C}[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j_{1}+1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2j_{2}+1})
:

(4.17)

\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{1})}_{{\langle 12\rangle}}\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{-j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{R}_{23}^{(j_{1}-\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j_{1})}_{{\langle 12\rangle}}\ ,

where

(4.18)

\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\ .

Proof.

First we show (
4.18
). By definition we have
\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(u)=(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes{\text{id}})({\bf L}^{(j_{2})}(u))
, therefore the application of
(\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes{\text{id}})
on (
4.14
) yields
11
11

11

The shifting of the labels
\{0,1,2\}
to
\{1,2,3\}
is due to the convention that the first tensor component of
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)
is labeled by
0
.

(
4.18
).
The eq. (
4.17
) is shown similarly to Proposition
4.5
using now (
R2
) and (
3.19
). It gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(v/w)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{(j_{1})}\mathcal{E}^{(j_{1})}\otimes{\text{id}}\bigr)\circ(\pi^{j_{1}}_{v}\otimes\pi^{j_{2}}_{w})(\mathfrak{R})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{1})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\left[(\pi_{vq^{-j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{vq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{w}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23})\right]\mathcal{E}^{(j_{1})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

and using the evaluation of the universal R-matrix from (
4.7
), the result follows after substituting
v/w\rightarrow u
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Recall that
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is not uniquely determined, see Section
3.2
. From the construction in the proof of Proposition
4.5
, it is clear that taking different expressions for
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
yields the same L-operators and R-matrices.

4.1.3.
L-operators and reduction
(\frac{1}{2},j)\rightarrow(j-\frac{1}{2})
.

We now consider quantum loop submodules of spin-
(j-\frac{1}{2})
and the corresponding
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
-intertwiner
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
in (
3.67
).
With the intertwining property (
3.31
),
we can write:

(1\otimes\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})})\left[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u}^{j-\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R})\right]=\left[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{j+1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{uq^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})\circ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{R})\right](1\otimes\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})})\ ,

with the convention discussed below (
3.62
). Then,
the proof of the following proposition essentially repeats the one of Proposition
4.5
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The L-operators (
4.1
) satisfy for
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
the following matrix relations with coefficient in
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-1}]]
:

(4.19)

{\bf L}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\bf L}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}){\bf L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j-1})\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}.

4.1.4.
P-symmetry of spin-
j
L-operators

We now give our first application of the fusion formula in Proposition
4.5
to show the so-called P-symmetry property of the spin-
j
L-operators, in (
4.23
) below. As a consequence, we show that the P-symmetry

(4.20)

\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)\ ,

with

(4.21)

\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)=\mathcal{P}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}\mathcal{R}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)\mathcal{P}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}\ ,

holds for any
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
, for the case of
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. Note that a proof of this equation for R-matrices can be found in
[
RSV14
, Lem. 2.1]
. Here, we give a different proof by showing first a more general relation

(4.22)

(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}^{-1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}^{-1}})(\mathfrak{R})=(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})\ ,

which can be interpreted as the P-symmetry on the level of L-operators. Indeed, identifying the l.h.s. of (
4.22
) with the spin-
j
L-operator defined by (
4.1
), the equation (
4.22
) reads

(4.23)

\displaystyle{\bf L}^{(j)}(u_{2}/u_{1})

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{ev}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})\ .

The proof of this equation is done by induction on
j
.
It is straightforward to check that (
4.23
) holds for
j=\frac{1}{2}
, by a calculation similar to the evaluation of
\mathfrak{R}
in Appendix
B
.
Now assume (
4.23
) holds for a fixed value of
j
, we show it holds for
(j+\frac{1}{2})
. It is done by identifying the r.h.s. of (
4.23
) with (
4.14
). Indeed, by an analysis similar to the proof of Proposition
4.5
, using the pseudo-inverse property
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}={\text{id}}
, (
3.37
) and (
2.11
), one gets:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\left[(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}q^{j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi^{j}_{u_{2}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}})(\mathfrak{R}_{31}\mathfrak{R}_{21})\right](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})

(4.24)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}{\bf L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u_{2}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}/u_{1}){\bf L}_{1}^{(j)}(u_{2}q^{j}/u_{1})\mathcal{E}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

where we used the assumption (
4.23
) for a fixed
j
to get the last line. Then, comparing (
4.24
) with (
4.14
), one finds that indeed
(\mathsf{ev}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})={\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u_{2}/u_{1})
.

Now, by specializing the first tensor component of (
4.23
) on the spin-
j_{1}
representation and for
j=j_{2}
, one obtains

(4.25)

\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{2}/u_{1})=(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})\ .

Finally, the immediate corollary of the latter relation is the P-symmetry (
4.20
).
Indeed, the R-matrix defined in (
4.21
) can be interpreted as the evaluation of the flipped universal R-matrix

(4.26)

\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{2}/u_{1})=(\pi^{j_{1}}_{u_{1}}\otimes\pi^{j_{2}}_{u_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})\ ,

and thus (
4.20
) holds.

4.1.5.
Fused L-operators and fused R-matrices

For further technical needs, let us introduce
a higher spin generalization of
2\times 2
matrices
\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
from (
4.2
).
For any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we define
the fused
L-operators
\cal{L}^{(j)}(u)\in U_{q}sl_{2}[u,u^{-1}]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
as:

(4.27)

{\cal L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u):=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\cal L}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}){\cal L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}.

Although not needed here, it can be proven directly by induction that
\cal{L}^{(j)}(u)
’s satisfy the Yang-Baxter equation (
4.10
), where
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)
are replaced by
{\cal L}^{(j)}(u)
.
We now give the relations between the spin-
j
L-operators (
4.1
), obtained by evaluation of the universal R-matrix, and the fused L-operators (
4.27
).

Lemma 4.9
.

The spin-
j
L-operators and the fused L-operators are related as follows:

(4.28)

{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)=\mu^{(j)}(u){\cal L}^{(j)}(u)\ ,

where

(4.29)

\mu^{(j)}(u)=\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-1}\mu(uq^{j-\frac{1}{2}-k})}\

is central in
U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-1}]]
.

Proof.

The relation (
4.28
) is shown by induction on
j
using (
4.14
) and (
4.27
) and the fact that
\mu(u)
is central in
U_{q}sl_{2}
. The first step of the induction is given by (
4.2
).
∎

As pointed out in
[
BGKNR12
]
, using the explicit expresssion (
B.34
)
one can show that the “normalising” generating function
\mu(u)
in (
4.2
), given by (
4.3
), satisfies the following functional relation:

(4.30)

\mu(u)\mu(uq)\gamma(u)=1\ ,

with
\gamma(u)
introduced in (
4.12
).
Actually, this functional relation can be independently derived from the fusion and reduction relations obtained in Propositions
4.5
and
4.8
without the knowledge of (
4.3
), as we now show.

Proposition 4.10
.

Assume the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator is of the form (
4.2
) for some formal Laurent series
\mu(u)
from
Z(U_{q}sl_{2})((u^{-1}))
. Then, the functional relation (
4.30
) holds.

Proof.

Comparing
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mu(u)\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
with (
4.19
) for
j=1
it follows:

(4.31)

\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mu(uq^{-1})\mu(uq^{-2})\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(1)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{1}(uq^{-2})\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(\frac{1}{2})}\ ,

where we used
\mu^{(1)}(u)
in (
4.29
).
For the r.h.s. of (
4.31
), after a direct calculation we find that

\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(1)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}_{1}(uq^{-2})\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(\frac{1}{2})}=\gamma(uq^{-2})\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\ .

Then, since
\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is invertible by (
4.13
), the relation (
4.30
) follows.
∎

We obtain the following corollary of the functional relation (
4.30
).

Corollary 4.11
.

(i)

The inverse of
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is given by

(4.32)

[{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)]^{-1}=-\mu(uq^{-1})\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1})\ .

(ii)

The quantum determinant of the L-operator
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is such that

(4.33)

{\text{tr}}_{12}\big(\mathcal{P}_{12}^{-}{\bf L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\bf L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq)\big)=1\ ,

where
\mathcal{P}^{-}
is given below (
4.12
).

Proof.

Recall first from (
4.3
) and Remark
4.4
that
\mu(u)
and
\gamma(u)
are invertible. Then, to prove the first statement we use
the first equality in (
4.2
), (
4.13
) and (
4.30
). Equation (
4.33
) follows directly from the first equality in (
4.2
), (
4.12
) and the functional relation (
4.30
).
∎

Remark 4.12
.

Assuming that
\mu(u)
in (
4.2
) is an invertible Laurent series in
u
with coefficients in a commutative subalgebra of
U_{q}sl_{2}
, its explicit form (
4.3
) can be derived, up to a sign, from the quadratic functional equation (
4.30
). Indeed, we can write
\mu(u)=\mu_{0}u^{-p}g(u)
for some scalar
\mu_{0}
and positive integer
p
, and
g(u)
is an invertible power series with coefficients in a commutative subalgebra of
U_{q}sl_{2}
with constant term
g_{0}=1
. Using (
4.12
), the relation (
4.30
) fixes
p=1
and
\mu_{0}=\pm q^{-1/2}
. Therefore,
\mu(u)=\pm u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}g(u)
where by (
4.30
)
g(u)
satisfies

(4.34)

\displaystyle g(u)g(uq)\xi(u)=1\qquad\mbox{with}\quad\xi(u)=1-Cu^{-2}q^{-2}+u^{-4}q^{-4}\ .

Equating the l.h.s. of the first equation in (
4.34
) with the one for
u
replaced by
uq^{-1}
is equivalent to
g(uq)\xi(u)=g(uq^{-1})\xi(uq^{-1})
. Inserting
g(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}g_{k}u^{-k}
in the last equation, we get the following three-term recurrence relation:

(4.35)

\displaystyle g_{k}=-\frac{1}{(q^{k}-q^{-k})}\left(-(q^{k}-q^{-k+2})Cq^{-2}g_{k-2}+(q^{k}-q^{-k+4})q^{-4}g_{k-4}\right)\ ,

with initial conditions
g_{0}=1
and
g_{k}=0
for all
k<0
. It gives
g_{1}=0
, which implies
g_{2\ell+1}=0
for all
\ell\geq 0
. Thus,
g(u)\in U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-2}]]
and by assumption
[g_{2k},g_{2\ell}]=0
for all
\ell,k>0
. As every formal power series can be written in an exponential form using the Bell polynomials
12
12

12

Note that
g_{2k}=\frac{B_{k}(q^{-1}\Lambda_{1},...,k!q^{-k}\Lambda_{k})}{k!}
, where
B_{k}(x_{1},...,x_{k})
are the complete Bell polynomials.
, let us define
g(u)=\exp(\Lambda(u^{-2}q^{-1}))
with
\Lambda(u)=\sum_{k=1}\Lambda_{k}u^{k}
. Now, taking the logarithm of the relation (
4.34
) with shifted argument
u\rightarrow uq^{-1}
, we get the relation:

(4.36)

\Lambda(u^{-2}q)+\Lambda(u^{-2}q^{-1})=-\log(1-Cu^{-2}+u^{-4})\ .

Introduce the central elements
C_{k}\in Z(U_{q}sl_{2})
defined by (
B.13
) in order to rewrite the r.h.s as the power series

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}C_{k}\frac{u^{-2k}}{k}
. Comparing the coefficients of the power series on both sides of the relation above determines uniquely
\Lambda_{k}=\frac{C_{k}}{(q^{k}+q^{-k})k}
, which concludes the proof.

We now introduce fused R-matrices by analogy with (
4.17
) and (
4.18
):

(4.37)

\displaystyle R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{1})}_{{\langle 12\rangle}}R_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{-j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}})R_{23}^{(j_{1}-\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j_{1})}_{{\langle 12\rangle}}\ ,

for
j_{1}\geq 1
and where

(4.38)

\displaystyle R^{(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}R_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})R_{12}^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\ ,

with
R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)
given in the right part of (
4.8
), and show that (
4.38
) indeed agrees with the explicit expression for
R^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)
given in (
3.41
).

Lemma 4.13
.

The R-matrices (
4.7
) and the fused R-matrices (
4.37
) are related by

(4.39)

\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)=f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)\ ,

where

(4.40)

f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)=u^{-4j_{1}j_{2}}q^{-2j_{1}j_{2}}\exp\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{q^{2k}+q^{-2k}}{1+q^{2k}}[2j_{1}]_{q^{k}}[2j_{2}]_{q^{k}}\frac{u^{-2k}}{k}\right)\ ,

and (
4.38
) agrees with (
3.41
).

Proof.

Firstly, we prove (
4.39
) for
j_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
. We show by induction on
j_{2}
that

(4.41)

\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(u)=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu^{(j_{2})}(u))\,R^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(u)\ ,

and we identify
\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu^{(j_{2})})(u)
with
f^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(u)
. For
j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, it is given in (
4.8
). Now, assuming (
4.41
) holds for a fixed value of
j_{2}
, we show it holds for
j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}
. Inserting (
4.18
) and (
4.38
) in (
4.41
) for
j_{2}\rightarrow j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}
and using (
4.29
), one finds that the equality indeed holds. Then, using (
4.29
) and (
4.9
) we find that

(4.42)

\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu^{(j_{2})})(u)=u^{-2j_{2}}q^{-j_{2}}\exp\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{q^{2k}+q^{-2k}}{1+q^{2k}}[2j_{2}]_{q^{k}}\frac{u^{-2k}}{k}\right)\ ,

and it coincides with (
4.40
) for
j_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Secondly, we show that (
4.38
) agrees with (
3.41
). On one hand, using (
4.2
) it is straightforward to find that (recall the notation in (
4.21
))

(4.43)

\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j,\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{P}^{(j,\frac{1}{2})}[(\pi^{j}\otimes{\text{id}})({\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u))]\mathcal{P}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}

is proportional to (
3.41
). On the other hand, from the P-symmetry in (
4.20
), one has
\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j,\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u)
is equally proportional to (
3.41
), as well as the expression in (
4.38
), recall (
4.41
). Finally, comparing the matrix entry
(1,1)
of (
3.41
) and (
4.38
), one finds that they are equal.

Thirdly, assuming that
\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
is proportional to
R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
as in (
4.39
), we show that
f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
takes the form (
4.40
).
Replacing the R-matrices and the fused R-matrices in (
4.39
) by (
4.17
), (
4.37
), and using (
4.41
) one gets:

\displaystyle f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu^{(j_{2})}(uq^{-j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}}))f^{(j_{1}-\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})

(4.44)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j_{1}-1}}\left[\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mu^{(j_{2})}(uq^{j_{1}-\frac{1}{2}-k}))\right]\ ,

where we set
f^{(0,j_{2})}(u)=1
. Finally, using (
4.42
) in the latter relation, one finds that
f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
is indeed given by (
4.40
) and so the claim follows.
∎

As a consequence of the above Lemma
4.13
, we see that the P-symmetry in (
1.4
) follows from (
4.20
) with the fact that
f^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)=f^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)
and that
\mu(u)
is invertible.

4.1.6.
Unitarity properties of fused L-operators

Later in the text, we will need various relations satisfied by the L-operators and R-matrices. They are obtained from the action of the quantum loop algebra (on tensor products) defined by the opposite coproduct, recall Remark
3.3
.

Lemma 4.14
.

The following relations hold:

(4.45)

\displaystyle{\bf L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\bf L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j}){\bf L}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle},

(4.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j})\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(j_{1},j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle},

(4.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(j+\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{R}_{23}^{(j,j_{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{R}_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ .

Proof.

Recall the discussion in Remark
3.3
about the action on tensor product of evaluation representations given by the opposite coproduct. The same comments also apply to the tensor product of quantum loop modules studied in Section
3.5
. In particular, the intertwining property (
3.37
) holds also at the level of formal
u
.
Now, combining (
3.37
) with
({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op})(\mathfrak{R})=\mathfrak{R}_{12}\mathfrak{R}_{13}
, which is obtained by applying
\mathfrak{p}_{23}
to (
R3
), then (
4.45
) is proven similarly to Proposition
4.5
. Eq. (
4.46
) follows from (
4.45
) by specialization of the 1st tensor component to the spin-
j_{1}
representation. The last relation (
4.47
) is proven similarly to (
4.17
) but using (
3.37
) and
(\Delta^{op}\otimes{\text{id}})(\mathfrak{R})=\mathfrak{R}_{23}\mathfrak{R}_{13}
, coming from the application of
\mathfrak{p}_{12}
to (
R2
), instead of (
3.19
) and (
R2
).
∎

Let us note that, using Remark
4.4
, the L-operator (
4.2
) satisfies the unitarity property

(4.48)

\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1})=-\gamma(uq^{-1})\mathbb{I}_{2}\ .

We now study spin-
j
generalization of this property starting with
j=1
. First, we need another form of the RLL equation (
4.10
):

(4.49)

\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2}){\bf L}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1}){\bf L}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})={\bf L}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2}){\bf L}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

which is found by applying
(\mathsf{ev}_{u}\otimes\pi^{j_{1}}_{v}\otimes\pi^{j_{2}}_{w})\circ\mathfrak{p}_{23}
to the universal Yang-Baxter equation (
2.3
), using (
4.26
) and substituting
v\rightarrow u/u_{1}
,
w\rightarrow u/u_{2}
, where we also used the P-symmetry property (
4.25
).
Recall from Lemma
4.9
that
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)
is proportional to
\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u)
, and so we also have

(4.50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

(4.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

where we used (
4.45
) for 2nd equality and the fact that all
\mu^{(j)}(u)
are invertible.
Recall that the fused L-operators are defined in (
4.27
). This expression matches the fusion relation (
4.51
) because, due to Lemma
4.9
, both follow from the fusion relations satisfied by the spin-
j
L-operators in (
4.14
) and (
4.45
). Indeed, comparing the two fusion relations written in terms of fused L-operators
\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u)
, the coefficients
\mu^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mu(uq^{j})
and
\mu^{(j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mu(uq^{-j})
on both sides cancel each other, as they are both equal to
\mu^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)
, see (
4.29
). Now, using (
4.27
) and (
4.51
) for
j=\frac{1}{2}
, we get

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u)\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u^{-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ .

Then, we need to use (
4.48
) to show that (
4.52
) is proportional to the identity matrix. However, there is an unwanted product
\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}
which is removed as follows. We recall first Corollary
3.6
, then insert
\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\Bigl[\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\Bigr]^{-1}=\mathbb{I}_{3}
in the r.h.s. of (
4.52
) and use (
3.48
), which gives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u)\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u^{-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(\tfrac{1}{u}q^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(\tfrac{1}{u}q^{\frac{1}{2}})R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(q)\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\bigl[\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\Bigr]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(q)\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(\tfrac{1}{u}q^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(\tfrac{1}{u}q^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\Bigl[\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\Bigr]^{-1}

(4.53)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{L}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

where we used the RLL equation given in (
4.50
) to get the second line, and the property (
3.50
), the RLL equation and (
3.48
) to get the third line. Finally, using (
4.48
), we get

\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u^{-1})\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u)=\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u)\mathcal{L}^{(1)}(u^{-1})=\gamma(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\gamma(uq^{-\frac{3}{2}})\mathbb{I}_{3}\ .

More generally, by induction one gets the unitarity property for any spin-
j
:

(4.54)

\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u^{-1})\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u)=\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u)\mathcal{L}^{(j)}(u^{-1})=\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{k=0}^{2j-1}}-\gamma(uq^{-j-\frac{1}{2}+k})\right)\mathbb{I}_{2j+1}\ .

Therefore, because the evaluation representation of the fused L-operators gives R-matrices, we also have

(4.55)

R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u^{-1})=R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u^{-1})R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)\propto\mathbb{I}_{(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)}\ ,

where the proportionality coefficient is a Laurent polynomial in
u
.

4.2.
Spin-
j
K-operators

Here, we introduce analogs of spin-
j
L-operators, that we call spin-
j
K-operators, and show that they satisfy the reflection equation (
1.8
) together with fusion and reduction relations.

As always, we consider the case
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
as introduced in Section
2.4
, however,
without specifying its comodule algebra
B
.
Assume that a universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}
exists for a choice of
B
and the twist pair
(\psi,J)=(\eta,1\otimes 1)

where
\eta
is defined in (
2.34
).
Similarly to the situation with the universal R-matrix
\mathfrak{R}
for
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, by this assumption we actually mean that there exists a “root vector” basis in
B
such that
\mathfrak{K}
can be formally written as a possibly infinite sum of products of the root vectors, i.e. each term in this sum is an element in
B\otimes\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. In particular, a basis of root vectors for
B=O_{q}
was constructed in
[
BK17
]
and for
B=\mathcal{A}_{q}
in
[
T21c
]
.
With this assumption, we
can consider evaluation of
\mathfrak{K}
on the second tensor component:

Definition 4.15
.

For
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
, introduce

(4.56)

{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)=({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j})(\mathfrak{K})\quad\in B((u^{-1}))\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})\ .

We call
{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)
the spin-
j
K-operator.

Similarly to the case of L-operators, recall Definition
4.1
and comments below it, we consider here
u
as a formal variable. In contrast to the L-operators, we do not have examples of explicit evaluations of
\mathfrak{K}
, and that is why in the above definition we assumed that the entries of K-operators are in the ring of formal Laurent series
B((u^{-1}))
instead of power series
B[[u^{-1}]]
. However, in the case of
B={\cal A}_{q}
we will see by Conjecture
1
in Section
6
that
{\bf K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is in
B[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, compare also with the similar Appel-Vlaar’s result
[
AV22
]
on 1-leg universal K-matrices for
B=O_{q}
.

By Proposition
2.11
, the universal K-matrix
\mathfrak{K}
satisfies the
\psi
-twisted reflection equation (
2.28
).
We now show that the evaluation of this equation leads to the reflection equation (
1.8
).
To do so, we need evaluation of the
\psi
-twisted universal R-matrices (
2.19
). Firstly, note that
\psi=\eta
from (
2.34
) is such that
(recall the definition in (
2.40
))

(4.57)

\mathsf{ev}_{u}\circ\eta=\mathsf{ev}_{u^{-1}}\ .

Then the evaluations of the
\psi
-twisted universal R-matrices read:

(4.58)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(1/(u_{1}u_{2}))\ ,

(4.59)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})((\mathfrak{R}^{\eta})_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(1/(u_{1}u_{2}))\ ,

(4.60)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})((\mathfrak{R}_{21})^{\eta})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}u_{2})\ ,

(4.61)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta\eta}_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

(4.62)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta\eta})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{2}/u_{1})\ ,

where
\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u)
is defined in (
4.21
).
Applying
\mathfrak{p}_{23}
to (
2.28
) leads to

\mathfrak{K}_{13}(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta})_{23}\mathfrak{K}_{12}\mathfrak{R}_{32}=\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\eta\eta}\mathfrak{K}_{12}(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}
. Finally, applying
({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u_{1}^{-1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}^{-1}}^{j_{2}})
to the latter equation using (
4.58
)-(
4.62
), it follows that the K-operator (
4.56
) satisfies the reflection equation
13
13

13

As for the L-operator
{\bf L}^{(j)}(u)
,
the K-operator should be written as
{\bf K}^{(j)}_{0i}(u)
but here we use standard notation
{\bf K}_{i}^{(j)}(u)
where we omit the label
0
corresponding to
B
.

(4.63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}u_{2}){\bf K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})=

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})\mathcal{R}_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2}){\bf K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}_{21}^{(j_{2},j_{1})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

for any value of
j_{1},j_{2}
.

Due to the P-symmetry (
4.20
), the relations (
4.59
)-(
4.61
) become

(4.64)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})((\mathfrak{R}^{\eta})_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(1/(u_{1}u_{2}))\ ,

(4.65)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})((\mathfrak{R}_{21})^{\eta})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2})\ ,

(4.66)

\displaystyle(\pi_{u_{1}}^{j_{1}}\otimes\pi_{u_{2}}^{j_{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{\eta\eta}_{21})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

and the reflection equation (
4.63
) becomes the standard reflection equation

(4.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2}){\bf K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})=

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{2}^{(j_{2})}(u_{2})\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}u_{2}){\bf K}_{1}^{(j_{1})}(u_{1})\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ ,

where we can also replace
\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
by
R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
due to Lemma
4.13
.

4.2.1.
K-operators and fusion
(\frac{1}{2},j)\rightarrow(j+\frac{1}{2})
.

We follow here the same approach used for L-operators based on sub-representations in the tensor product of formal evaluation representations of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, now considering (
K3
) instead of (
R3
).
Recall the intertwining operator
{\mathcal{E}}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed by Lemma (
3.1
) and its pseudo-inverse
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is given in (
3.25
) with (
3.26
) and (
3.27
).
We now obtain our first main result.

Proposition 4.16
.

The K-operators (
4.56
) satisfy for
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
:

(4.68)

{\bf K}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\bf K}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u^{2}q^{j-\frac{1}{2}}){\bf K}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}.

Proof.

By definition of the K-operator we have

{\bf K}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{K})\ .

Using the pseudo-inverse property
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}=\mathbb{I}_{2j+2}
, we get

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{K})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{K})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})\circ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{K})](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})(\mathfrak{K}_{13}\mathfrak{R}_{23}^{\eta}\mathfrak{K}_{12})](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\ .

The second equality is obtained using the intertwining property (
3.19
):

(4.69)

(1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{j+\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{K})=[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{-j}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{j})\circ({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta)(\mathfrak{K})](1\otimes\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})})\ .

Then, the third equality is due to (
K3
) and finally, from the definition of the K-operator (
4.56
) and the evaluation of the twisted universal R-matrix (
4.58
), the claim follows.
∎

We see from the above Proposition
4.16
that
{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)
is a formal power series in
u^{-1}
, i.e. it is in
B[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
, whenever
{\bf K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is so.

Remark 4.17
.

Similarly to Remark
4.7
, it is clear from the proof of Proposition
4.16
that the K-operator does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{F}^{(j)}
, as long as it satisfies
\mathcal{F}^{(j)}\mathcal{E}^{(j)}=\mathbb{I}_{2j+1}
.

4.2.2.
K-operators and reduction
(\frac{1}{2},j)\rightarrow(j-\frac{1}{2})
.

The proof of the following proposition is done similarly to the proof of the reduction relation (
4.19
) for the L-operators, thus we skip it.
Recall the intertwining operator
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed by Lemma
3.2
and its pseudo-inverse
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
is given in (
3.33
) with (
3.34
).

Proposition 4.18
.

The K-operators (
4.56
) satisfy for
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
:

(4.70)

{{\bf K}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}{\bf K}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u^{2}q^{-j-\frac{3}{2}}){\bf K}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j-1})\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}\ .

Recall that we assumed that the universal K-matrix exists for a given choice of
B
and the twist pair
(\eta,1\otimes 1)
. Therefore, the K-operator for a given spin is unique,
that is, similarly to the case of the L-operator, we obtain the same operator
{\bf K}^{(j)}(u)
either using the fusion for
(\frac{1}{2},j-\frac{1}{2})\rightarrow j
or using the reduction
(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})\rightarrow j
.

Remark 4.19
.

Consider the opposite coproduct
\Delta^{op}=\mathfrak{p}\circ\Delta
with the definition (
A.9
). It follows from (
K3
):

(4.71)

({\text{id}}\otimes\Delta^{op})(\mathfrak{K})=\mathfrak{K}_{12}(\mathfrak{R}^{\psi})_{32}\mathfrak{K}_{13}\ .

Recall that we obtained for
\Delta^{op}
an intertwining relation in (
3.37
) where
\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
is fixed as in (
3.20
), see Remark
3.3
. Thus, we also take
\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}
as defined in (
3.26
), (
3.27
). Then, using (
3.37
) and (
4.71
), we obtain a new fusion relation for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
:

(4.72)

{\bf K}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{F}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}{\bf K}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j})\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u^{2}q^{-j+\frac{1}{2}}){\bf K}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle 12\rangle}.

Similarly, we also have the intertwining relation for
\Delta^{op}
given in (
3.36
)
with
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
fixed as in (
3.32
), see Remark
3.3
, and we take
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}
as defined in (
3.33
) with (
3.34
). Then, using (
4.71
) and (
3.38
), we obtain a new reduction relation for any
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
:

(4.73)

{\bf K}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}{\bf K}_{1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j+1})\mathcal{R}^{(\frac{1}{2},j)}(u^{2}q^{j+\frac{3}{2}}){\bf K}_{2}^{(j)}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{\langle 12\rangle}^{(j-\frac{1}{2})}.

To conclude this subsection, let us comment on the other choice of twist pair
(\psi,J)=(\eta,\mathfrak{R}_{21}\mathfrak{R})
from Example
2.13
. The universal reflection equation (
2.28
) with
\psi=\eta
produces the same reflection equation (
4.67
) as with the first choice of the twist pair. However, the fusion formula based on the axiom (
K3
) requires evaluating
J
on tensor product of evaluation representations. Since this evaluation might not converge, due to infinite sums of Casimir eigenvalues, we do not consider it in detail.

4.3.
Comodule algebra structure using K-operators

Given the Hopf algebra
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
, it is known that the coproduct, antipode and counit can be formulated solely in terms of so-called Ding-Frenkel L-operators
[
DF93
]
:

(4.74)

\mathbf{L}^{+}(u)=({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R})\ ,\qquad\mathbf{L}^{-}(u)=[({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}_{21})]^{-1}\ ,

They are computed in Appendix
B
, see (
B.26
) and (
B.33
).
Then, one finds that
{\bf L}^{\pm}(u)
are formal power series in
u^{\mp 1}
, i.e.
{\bf L}^{\pm}(u)
are in
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{\mp 1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
The modes of the entries of
{\bf L}^{\pm}(u)
generate
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and they satisfy the Yang-Baxter algebra relations:

(4.75)

\displaystyle R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u/v)\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{1}(u)\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{2}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{2}(v)\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{1}(u)R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u/v)\ ,

(4.76)

\displaystyle R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u/v)\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{1}(u)\mathbf{L}^{\mp}_{2}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}^{\mp}_{2}(v)\mathbf{L}^{\pm}_{1}(u)R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u/v)\ ,

with
R^{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}(u)
defined in (
4.8
).
These four relations are obtained by applying
({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\pi_{v^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})
to the following four equations:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{23}\eqref{YB-noparam}\ ,\qquad
