∎

Proposition 6.12
.

Assume Conjecture
1
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}^{(j)}(u)
from (
5.31
) is equal to the fused K-operator
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
defined in (
5.29
).

Proof.

Recall the K-operators
{\bf K}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)
can be written either as (
4.68
) or as (
4.72
). Using (
6.1
) with (
6.2
) and the invertibility of
\nu^{(j)}(u)
, we show by induction (recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\mathcal{K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
) that
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}(u)
equals
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}^{(j)}(u)
.
∎

7.
K-operators and the PBW basis

As mentioned in the introduction, in the literature the method (ii) has been successfully applied to the derivation of K-matrices with scalar entries solely using twisted intertwining relations in various representations of certain comodule algebras
B
. In this section, solutions
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
of the twisted intertwining relation (
5.55
) are investigated using a PBW basis of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
for
j=\frac{1}{2},1
. We show that for a reasonably general ansatz, the solutions are uniquely determined (up to an overall factor), and match with the expressions of
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
’s for
j=\frac{1}{2}
and
j=1
constructed in Section
5
.

Different types of PBW bases for
{\cal A}_{q}
are known
[
T21a
]
.
For instance, a PBW basis for
{\cal A}_{q}
can be constructed in terms of the PBW generators

\{{\mathsf{W}}_{-k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\ ,\quad\{{\mathsf{G}}_{\ell+1}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}\ ,\quad\{\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{m+1}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\ ,\quad\{{\mathsf{W}}_{n+1}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

in the linear order
<
that satisfies
17
17

17

For a different choice of ordering, the proof of the PBW basis is given in
[
T21a
]
.

(7.1)

{\mathsf{W}}_{-k}<{\mathsf{G}}_{\ell+1}<\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{m+1}<{\mathsf{W}}_{n+1}\ ,\qquad k,\ell,m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ .

We recall that the corresponding generating functions
\cal W_{\pm}(u)
and
\cal G_{\pm}(u)
were introduced in (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), respectively.

In the following analysis, we will encounter various combinations of the generating functions
\cal W_{\pm}(u)
and
\cal G_{\pm}(u)
that will need reordering. According to the chosen order (
7.1
), any words in
\{\cal W_{\pm}(u_{i}),\cal G_{\pm}(u_{i})\}_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
can be written in terms of ordered expressions using Lemma
C.1
.
In particular, from the relations (
C.1
)-(
C.6
), one extracts exchange relations that will be used later on for solving the twisted intertwining relations:

(7.2)

\displaystyle\cal W_{+}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{W}}_{0}\cal W_{+}(u)\ ,\qquad{\mathsf{W}}_{1}\cal W_{-}(u)=\cal W_{-}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{1}\ ,

\displaystyle\cal G_{+}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=q^{2}{\mathsf{W}}_{0}\cal G_{+}(u)+\rho q({\mathsf{W}}_{0}+\cal W_{-}(u)-U\cal W_{+}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle\cal G_{-}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=q^{-2}{\mathsf{W}}_{0}\cal G_{-}(u)-\rho q^{-1}({\mathsf{W}}_{0}+\cal W_{-}(u)-U\cal W_{+}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle\cal W_{-}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{W}}_{0}\cal W_{-}(u)+\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}(\cal G_{+}(u)-\cal G_{-}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{1}\cal G_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=q^{2}\cal G_{+}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{1}+\rho q({\mathsf{W}}_{1}+\cal W_{+}(u)-U\cal W_{-}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{1}\cal G_{-}(u)

\displaystyle=q^{-2}\cal G_{-}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{1}-\rho q^{-1}({\mathsf{W}}_{1}+\cal W_{+}(u)-U\cal W_{-}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{1}\cal W_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\cal W_{+}(u){\mathsf{W}}_{1}+\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}(\cal G_{+}(u)-\cal G_{-}(u))\ .

From the construction of the fused K-operator
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
in (
5.29
), it is a
(2j+1)\times(2j+1)
matrix with entries in
{\cal A}_{q}[[u^{-1}]]
that are linear combinations of monomials of the form (recall the ordering (
7.1
))

(7.3)

f(u)\prod_{r=1}^{R}{\cal W}_{+}(uq^{a_{r}})\prod_{p=1}^{P}{\cal G}_{+}(uq^{b_{p}})\prod_{m=1}^{M}{\cal G}_{-}(uq^{c_{m}})\prod_{t=1}^{T}{\cal W}_{-}(uq^{d_{t}})\ ,\qquad R+P+M+T\leq 2j

and for some choice of
a_{r},b_{p},c_{m},d_{t}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, and
f(u)
is a Laurent polynomial in
u
of maximal degree
2(R+P+M+T)
.
Below, we successively show for the cases
j=\frac{1}{2}
and
j=1
that the twisted intertwining relations (
5.55
) admit a unique solution of the above form, and it agrees with
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
’s constructed in Section
5
.

Before proceeding, we first notice that it is enough to solve the relations (
5.55
) for
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0}
and
b={\mathsf{W}}_{1}
. This follows from the fact that
{\cal A}_{q}
is the central extension of
O_{q}
– it is generated by
{\mathsf{W}}_{0}
,
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
and its center. We can take the quantum determinant
\Gamma(u)
as the generating function of the center of
{\cal A}_{q}
, recall its definition in (
5.20
). This central element has a particularly simple expression (
5.49
) for the evaluated coaction
\delta_{v}
– both the tensor components are central and
\delta_{v}(\Gamma(u))=\delta_{v^{-1}}(\Gamma(u))
. From this observation, it is clear that the relations (
5.55
) hold for
b=\Gamma(u)
and whatever choice of
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
, that is, they do not give any constraints on the form of
{\cal K}^{(j)}(u)
.

7.1.
Spin-
\frac{1}{2}
K-operator

The above discussed ansatz for
{\cal K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
takes the form:

(7.4)

{\cal K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=A_{1,1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\cal W}_{+}(u)+A_{1,2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\cal W}_{-}(u)+A_{1,3}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\cal G}_{+}(u)+A_{1,4}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u){\cal G}_{-}(u)+A_{0}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\ ,

where
A_{1,i}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
and
A_{0}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
are two-by-two matrices. We are now solving the twisted intertwining relation (
5.55
) for
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
using the ordering relations (
7.2
).
For
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0}
it is straightforward to show using (
2.47
) that the twisted intertwining relation (
5.55
) is equivalent to the system of four equations:

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\cal K}_{11}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\right]=u^{-1}q^{-1}(k_{-}{\cal K}_{12}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)-k_{+}{\cal K}_{21}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\cal K}_{22}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\right]=-uq(k_{-}{\cal K}_{12}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)-k_{+}{\cal K}_{21}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\cal K}_{12}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\right]_{q}=k_{+}(u{\cal K}_{11}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)-u^{-1}{\cal K}_{22}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u))\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\cal K}_{21}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)\right]_{q^{-1}}=-k_{-}(u{\cal K}_{11}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)-u^{-1}{\cal K}_{22}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u))\ .

For
b={\mathsf{W}}_{1}
, the analogous system of equations is obtained by substituting
{\mathsf{W}}_{0}\mapsto{\mathsf{W}}_{1},u\mapsto u^{-1},q\mapsto q^{-1}
into the above equations.
Inserting the K-operator’s ansatz (
7.4
)
into these two sets of intertwining relations and using (
7.2
), one extracts a set of linearly independent equations that determine uniquely (up to an overall factor) the entries of the matrices
A_{1,i}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u),A_{0}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
. Adjusting the overall normalization for convenience, they read:

\displaystyle A_{1,1}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}uq&0\\
0&-u^{-1}q^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_{1,2}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}-u^{-1}q^{-1}&0\\
0&uq\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_{1,3}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{k_{-}(q+q^{-1})}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle A_{1,4}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\frac{1}{k_{+}(q+q^{-1})}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_{0}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{k_{+}(q+q^{-1})}{q-q^{-1}}\\
\frac{k_{-}(q+q^{-1})}{q-q^{-1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\ .\hskip 56.9055pt

Clearly, it is seen that the K-operator (
7.4
) matches with the fundamental K-operator (
5.4
).

7.2.
Spin-
1
fused K-operator

For
j=1
, an ansatz for the K-operator is built along the same line. Quadratic and linear combinations of the generating functions
\{\cal W_{\pm}(u),\cal G_{\pm}(u)\}
are now expected. Let
\alpha,\beta
be half-integers. The ansatz reads:

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(1)}(u)

\displaystyle=A_{2,1}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal W_{+}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,2}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal G_{+}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,3}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\beta})\cal G_{+}(uq^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+A_{2,4}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal G_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,5}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\beta})\cal G_{-}(uq^{\alpha})+A_{2,6}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\beta})

\displaystyle+A_{2,7}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\beta})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\alpha})+A_{2,8}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal G_{+}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,9}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal G_{-}(uq^{\beta})

\displaystyle+A_{2,10}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\beta})\cal G_{-}(uq^{\alpha})+A_{2,11}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\alpha})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,12}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\beta})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+A_{2,13}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{-}(uq^{\alpha})\cal G_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,14}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{-}(uq^{\alpha})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{2,15}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{-}(uq^{\beta})\cal W_{-}(uq^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+A_{2,16}^{(1)}(u)\mathcal{W}_{-}(uq^{\alpha})\mathcal{W}_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{1,1}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\alpha})+A_{1,2}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{+}(uq^{\beta})\!+\!A_{1,3}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+A_{1,4}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{+}(uq^{\beta})+A_{1,5}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{-}(uq^{\alpha})+A_{1,6}^{(1)}(u)\cal G_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{1,7}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{-}(uq^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+A_{1,8}^{(1)}(u)\cal W_{-}(uq^{\beta})+A_{0}^{(1)}\ ,

where
A_{i,j}^{(1)}(u)
and
A_{0}^{(1)}
are three-by-three matrices.
Again, assume the twisted intertwining relations (
5.55
) hold for
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
, then
for
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0}
we have:

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{11}^{(1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{-1}q^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}\left(k_{-}\mathcal{K}_{12}^{(1)}(u)-k_{+}\mathcal{K}_{21}^{(1)}(u)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{12}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(u^{-1}q^{-1}k_{-}\mathcal{K}_{13}^{(1)}(u)+k_{+}(u\mathcal{K}_{11}^{(1)}(u)-u^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{22}^{(1)}(u))\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{13}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{+}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}\left(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{K}_{12}^{(1)}(u)-u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{K}_{23}^{(1)}(u)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{21}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}q^{-\frac{3}{2}}u^{-1}\left(k_{-}q(\mathcal{K}_{22}^{(1)}(u)-u^{2}\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{31}(u))-k_{+}\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{31}(u)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{22}^{(1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(k_{+}(uq\mathcal{K}_{21}^{(1)}(u)-u^{-1}\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{32}(u))+k_{-}(u^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{23}^{(1)}(u)-uq\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{12}(u))\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{23}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}\left(k_{+}(u\mathcal{K}_{22}^{(1)}(u)-u^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{33}^{(1)}(u))-k_{-}uq\mathcal{K}_{13}^{(1)}(u)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{31}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q^{-2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{-}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}\left(u^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{K}_{32}^{(1)}(u)-uq^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{K}_{21}^{(1)}(u)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{32}^{(1)}(u)\right]_{q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}\left(k_{+}uq\mathcal{K}_{31}^{(1)}(u)-k_{-}(u\mathcal{K}_{22}^{(1)}(u)-u^{-1}\mathcal{K}_{33}^{(1)}(u))\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left[{\mathsf{W}}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{33}^{(1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle uq^{\frac{3}{2}}\sqrt{\left[2\right]_{q}}(k_{+}\mathcal{K}_{32}^{(1)}(u)-k_{-}\mathcal{K}_{23}^{(1)}(u))\ .

By substituting
{\mathsf{W}}_{0}\mapsto{\mathsf{W}}_{1},u\mapsto u^{-1},q\mapsto q^{-1}
into the above equations, we obtain the system of equations associated with
b={\mathsf{W}}_{1}
.
Inserting the K-operator’s ansatz (
7.6
) into these equations and using (
7.2
) we extract a system of linearly independent equations for the entries of
{\cal K}^{(1)}(u)
. Similarly to the case
j=\frac{1}{2}
, one finds that it determines uniquely
\alpha=\frac{1}{2},\beta=-\frac{1}{2}
and the matrices

and

are given by

A_{1,1}^{(1)}(u)=\frac{c(u^{2})(q+q^{-1})^{2}}{c(q)c(u^{2}q)}\begin{pmatrix}0&uq^{\frac{3}{2}}k_{+}\frac{(u^{2}q^{2}+u^{-2}q^{-2})}{\sqrt{q+q^{-1}}}&0\\

0&0&q\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad A_{2,8}^{(1)}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\frac{k_{+}c(u^{2})}{k_{-}\rho}\\

\end{pmatrix},

\end{pmatrix}\!,\ A_{2,3}^{(1)}(u)=\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}c(u^{2})}{uk_{-}c(u^{2}q)}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\sqrt{q+q^{-1}}&0\\

0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

A_{2,11}^{(1)}(u)=\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}c(u^{2})}{uk_{-}c(u^{2}q)}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{(u^{2}q^{2}+u^{-2}q^{-2})}{\sqrt{q+q^{-1}}}&0\\

\end{pmatrix}\!,\ A_{2,12}^{(1)}(u)\!=\!\frac{uq^{\frac{3}{2}}c(u^{2})}{k_{-}c(u^{2}q)}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\sqrt{q+q^{-1}}&0\\

\frac{k_{-}c(u^{2})}{k_{+}c(q)}&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle A_{2,16}^{(1)}(u)

\displaystyle=\text{Diag}(q^{-1}-u^{-4}q^{-3},-u^{2}q+u^{-2}(q+q^{-1}-q^{3}),u^{4}q^{3}-q^{3}+q^{-1}-q^{-3})\ ,

\displaystyle A_{2,6}^{(1)}(u)

\displaystyle=\text{Diag}(-c(u^{2}q)q,-u^{-4}q^{-2}-q^{2}+1+q^{-2},-c(u^{2}q)q)\ ,

A_{1,2}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,1}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}q^{\mp 2}}\ ,A_{1,7}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,1}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}u^{\mp 2}q^{\mp 3}}\ ,A_{1,8}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,1}^{(1)}(u)\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\pm}u^{\mp 2}q^{\mp 1}}\ ,

A_{1,4}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,3}^{(1)}(u^{-1})\rvert_{q\rightarrow q^{-1}}\ ,A_{1,5}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,3}^{(1)t}(u^{-1})\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp},q\rightarrow q^{-1}}\ ,A_{1,6}^{(1)}(u)=A_{1,3}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{+}\rightarrow k_{-}},

A_{2,1}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,16}^{(1)}(u^{-1})\rvert_{q\rightarrow-q^{-1}}\ ,A_{2,13}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,8}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{+}\rightarrow k_{-}}\ ,A_{2,4}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,3}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{-}\rightarrow k_{+}q^{2}}\ ,

A_{2,5}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,2}^{(1)t}(u)\rvert_{k_{-}\rightarrow k_{+}q^{2}}\ ,A_{2,7}^{(1)}(u)=-A_{2,6}^{(1)}(u^{-1})\rvert_{q\rightarrow q^{-1}}\ ,A_{2,9}^{(1)}(u)=-(\rho c(u^{2}q))^{-1}A_{2,6}^{(1)}(u)\ ,

A_{2,10}^{(1)}(u)=-(\rho c(u^{2}q))^{-1}A_{2,6}^{(1)}(u^{-1})\rvert_{q\rightarrow q^{-1}}\ ,A_{2,14}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,12}^{(1)}(u)\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}q^{2}}\ ,A_{2,15}^{(1)}(u)=A_{2,11}^{(1)}(u)\rvert_{k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}q^{2}}\ .

As expected, one checks that the K-operator in (
7.6
) together with the above solution matches with the expression for the fused K-operator (
5.37
) after applying the ordering relations given in Appendix
C
.

To summarize, K-operators of the form (
7.4
), (
7.6
) for
j=\frac{1}{2},1
, respectively, are uniquely determined (up to an overall factor) by the twisted intertwining relations (
5.55
). Importantly, it is sufficient to consider the relations for
b={\mathsf{W}}_{0},{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
.
Furthermore, the corresponding expressions match with the fused ones derived in Section
5
. Based on these evidences, we conjecture that spin-
j
fused K-operator solutions of (
5.55
) are unique (up to an overall scalar factor), their matrix entries are linear combinations of monomials of the form (
7.3
) and match with the fused expression given by (
5.29
).

8.
Summary and Outlook

To briefly summarize our main results, we provided a new set of K-operator solutions to the spectral parameter dependent reflection equation (
1.8
) in terms of generating functions of the centrally extended
q
-Onsager algebra
{\cal A}_{q}
.
The central formula of this work is the recursion (
5.29
) for the fused K-operators of arbitrary spin
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
as well as Theorem
5.7
on the reflection equation they satisfy.
We also gave formulas for the fused R-matrices and the fused K-operators in (
5.43
), (
5.44
), whose expressions contain only the fundamental R-matrix and K-operator. These results were established within a general framework of universal K-matrices that we developed in Section
2.3
, extending the previously known approaches (discussed in Introduction). In particular, the central formula (
5.29
) is based on the results in Proposition
4.16
and in Remark
4.19
. We also provided in Section
5.4
a few explicit examples of the fused K-operators (for spins
j=1
and
j=\frac{3}{2}
) in terms of generating functions of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
.

As the existence of a universal K-matrix (for our choice of algebras
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
and
B={\cal A}_{q}
and the compatible twists) is still an open fundamental question, we have investigated whether the central formula (
5.29
) satisfies the (evaluated version of) universal K-matrix axioms (
K1
)-(
K3
) which is resulted in Conjecture
1
. One of the key problems here is to understand better the central element
\nu(u)\in{\cal A}_{q}[[u^{-1}]]
, in particular to derive its coaction
\delta(\nu(u))
so that it reproduces the evaluated coaction in (
6.4
).

It is also important to mention a few possible applications of our results in integrable models. In the literature on quantum integrable systems, K-operators and their
images in the tensor product (or spin-chain) representations of the algebra
{\cal A}_{q}
– known as Sklyanin’s operators – are the
basic building elements for the construction of mutually commuting
quantities, for instance the Hamiltonian of open spin chains with
integrable boundary conditions
[
Sk88
]
. For the quotient of
{\cal A}_{q}
known as the
q
-Onsager algebra,
the fundamental K-operator (
5.4
) is the essential ingredient in the open XXZ spin-
\frac{1}{2}
chain with generic boundary
conditions
[
BK05b
]
. For the generic diagonal boundary conditions in this spin chain, the fundamental K-operator (
5.4
) generates another quotient
18
18

18

More precisely, it is a degenerate specialization at
\rho\to 0
of the
q
-Onsager quotient.
of
{\cal A}_{q}
known as the augmented
q
-Onsager algebra
[
BB12
, Sec. 2]
. Furthermore, for the quotient of
{\cal A}_{q}
known as the Askey-Wilson algebra, the K-operator (
5.4
)
leads to an integrable model with three-spin interaction terms
[
BP19
]
. For other cases, the corresponding quotients of
{\cal A}_{q}
appearing in the finite or half-infinite spin chains are identified in
[
BB16
, Sec. 3]
.

The K-operators play a crucial role in the spectrum analysis of the corresponding
quantum spin chains, for instance, in the diagonalization of the Hamiltonian of the half-infinite XXZ spin-chain with diagonal boundary conditions
[
JKKKM94
]
(see also
[
BB12
]
in Onsager’s approach) and with triangular ones
[
BB12
]
, or describe hidden non-abelian symmetries
[
BB16
]
of the model. The K-operators are also used to construct the Baxter’s
Q
-operator for diagonal boundary conditions
[
BT17
,
VW20
]
and triangular boundary conditions
[
Ts19
,
Ts20
]
.
For all these models, the transfer matrix is the image in the spin-chain representation of a generating function
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)

built from the K-operator (
5.4
) and a dual solution of the reflection equation
for a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
auxiliary space. Importantly,
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
reads as a

linear
combination of some fundamental generators
\{{\cal I}_{2k+1}|k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
of a maximal commutative subalgebra of
{\cal A}_{q}
. Therefore, in this approach the
diagonalization of the transfer matrix reduces to the diagonalization of
the image of the commutative subalgebra.

The fused K-operators of spin-
j
constructed in this paper open a route to the representation-independent analysis of related integrable models beyond the case of the fundamental spin-
\frac{1}{2}
auxiliary space, for instance, of the open XXZ spin-
j
chain with generic integrable boundary conditions.
And the following problems can be addressed here:

•

Firstly,
it is natural to ask about relations between any local
19
19

19

In the case of integrable spin chains,
an operator is said to be local whenever it is a product of a finite number of spin matrices
S_{\pm},S_{3}
, or a sum of such products.
or
non-local mutually commuting quantities of quantum spin chains and the generators
\{{\cal I}_{2k+1}|k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
of the commutative subalgebra in
{\cal A}_{q}
. For
instance, in the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
case the differentiation of the transfer matrix leads to the expression of the Hamiltonian in terms of the operators
{\cal I}_{2k+1}
’s
[
BK05b
, eq. (39)]
. We thus also expect that the transfer matrix for the models based on the auxiliary space of arbitrary spin-
j
admits a unified formulation
as the image of a generating function
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
in the commutative
subalgebra of
{\cal A}_{q}[[u^{-1}]]
. In a forthcoming paper
[
LBG24
]
, the
structure of the universal transfer matrices
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
for higher spin-
j
auxiliary space
representation will be studied in details. In particular, the so called TT-relations – a recursion on
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
given in (
1.14
) –
are derived at the algebraic level, independently of a
representation chosen. Generalizing the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
case, it will be shown
that
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
is a power series in
u^{-1}
with coefficients being polynomials of degree
2j
in the generators
\{{\cal I}_{2k+1}|k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
, and that the universal
{\bf{\mathsf{T}}}^{(j)}(u)
’s indeed agree with the ‘physical’ transfer matrices on the spin chains.

•

Secondly, given those transfer matrices generated from various images of

{\cal A}_{q}
, the problem of characterizing their spectral properties –
leading to the eigenstates and eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian – is consequently
reduced to the diagonalization of the images of
\{{\cal I}_{2k+1}|k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
. For the simplest example of the quotient of

{\cal A}_{q}
known as the Askey-Wilson algebra, for irreducible finite-dimensional representations the problem is solved in
[
BP19
]
, combining the theory
of Leonard pairs and the so-called modified algebraic Bethe ansatz
[
BC13
,
B14
,
BP14
]
. A similar analysis for
{\cal A}_{q}
remains to be
done.

•

Let us mention that the construction of a universal
Q
-operator
for
{\cal A}_{q}
and corresponding TQ-relations may be also addressed. In
that case, it would be desirable to construct the analogue of the fused
K-operator for
j\rightarrow\infty
as suggested in
[
YNZ05
]
, see also
[
VW20
,
Ts20
]
.

•

Finally, it is very desirable to construct K-operators of arbitrary complex spins, i.e., associated to
U_{q}sl_{2}
Verma modules of complex weights, as it would give essentially the corresponding universal K-matrix. Such integrable quantum spin-chains with integrable boundary conditions based on the Verma modules were recently introduced
[
CGS22
]
, and it was shown
[
CGJS22
]
a deep connection to the
q
-Onsager algebra via the common XXZ spin chain spectrum with the integrable non-diagonal boundary conditions.

Appendix A
Quantum algebras

We recall the definitions of the quantum algebra
U_{q}sl_{2}
and the quantum affine algebra
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
.

Definition A.1
.

The algebra
U_{q}sl_{2}
is a Hopf algebra generated by the elements
E,F,K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}
satisfying:

(A.1)

K^{\frac{1}{2}}E=qEK^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\quad K^{\frac{1}{2}}F=q^{-1}FK^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\quad[E,F]=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\ ,\quad K^{\frac{1}{2}}K^{-\frac{1}{2}}=K^{-\frac{1}{2}}K^{\frac{1}{2}}=1\ .

Let
\Delta\colon U_{q}sl_{2}\to U_{q}sl_{2}\otimes U_{q}sl_{2}
,
\epsilon\colon U_{q}sl_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
and
S\colon U_{q}sl_{2}\to U_{q}sl_{2}
be respectively the coproduct, the counit and the antipode. They are given by:

(A.2)

\displaystyle\Delta(E)=E\otimes K^{-\frac{1}{2}}+K^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes E\ ,\quad\Delta(F)=F\otimes K^{-\frac{1}{2}}+K^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes F\ ,\quad\Delta(K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\otimes K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\epsilon(E)=\epsilon(F)=0\ ,\qquad\epsilon(K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=1\ ,\hskip 113.81102pt

(A.4)

\displaystyle S(E)=-q^{-1}E\ ,\qquad S(F)=-qF\ ,\qquad S(K^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}\ .\hskip 56.9055pt

We also recall that the Casimir central element of
U_{q}sl_{2}
is given by

(A.5)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=(q-q^{-1})^{2}FE+qK+q^{-1}K^{-1}

\displaystyle=(q-q^{-1})^{2}EF+q^{-1}K+qK^{-1}\ .

Note that the monomials
\{E^{r}K^{\pm\frac{s}{2}}F^{t}\ |\ r,s,t\in\mathbb{N}\}
provide a PBW basis for
U_{q}sl_{2}
, see for instance
[
KS12
, Chap. 3]
.

Definition A.2
.

Define the extended Cartan matrix
a_{ij}
with
a_{ii}=2,\;a_{ij}=-2
for
i\neq j
.
The quantum affine algebra
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
is a Hopf algebra generated by the elements
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},\;i\in\{0,1\}
satisfying:

(A.6)

\displaystyle K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{j}=q^{\frac{a_{ij}}{2}}E_{j}K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{j}=q^{-\frac{a_{ij}}{2}}F_{j}K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad[E_{i},F_{j}]=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\ ,

(A.7)

\displaystyle K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}=K_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}=1\ ,\qquad K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}=K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\hskip 56.9055pt

with the
q
-Serre relations:

(A.8)

[E_{i},[E_{i},[E_{i},E_{j}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]=0\ ,\qquad[F_{i},[F_{i},[F_{i},F_{j}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}]=0\ .

Let
\Delta\colon U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}\to U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}\otimes U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
,
\epsilon\colon U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
and
S\colon U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}\to U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
be respectively the coproduct, the counit and the antipode. They are given by:

(A.9)

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}+K_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\otimes E_{i},\;\;\Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}+K_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\otimes F_{i},\;\;\Delta(K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\otimes K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},

(A.10)

\displaystyle\epsilon(E_{i})=\epsilon(F_{i})=0\ ,\qquad\epsilon(K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=1\ ,\hskip 125.19194pt

(A.11)

\displaystyle S(E_{i})=-qE_{i}\ ,\qquad S(F_{i})=-q^{-1}F_{i}\ ,\qquad S(K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}_{i}\ .\hskip 48.36958pt

The element
K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
is central. The algebra
U_{q}\widehat{sl}_{2}
has an automorphism
\nu
defined by

(A.12)

\nu(E_{i})=E_{i}K^{\frac{1}{2}}_{i}\ ,\qquad\nu(F_{i})=K^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{i}F_{i}\ ,\qquad\nu(K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}})=K_{i}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\ .

Appendix B
The universal R-matrix

In this appendix, we compute evaluations of the universal R-matrix.
Firstly, we recall the construction of the universal R-matrix of Khoroshkin-Tolstoy
[
KT92a
]
for the Hopf algebra
H=\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
in terms of root vectors. Secondly, evaluations of the universal R-matrix are considered. In particular, we give expressions of the Ding-Frenkel L-operators
\mathbf{L}^{+}(u)
and
[\mathbf{L}^{-}(u^{-1})]^{-1}
, as defined in (
4.74
). Finally, the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator
{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
is computed by evaluating
{\bf L}^{+}(u)
.

B.1.
Root vectors

Let us first recall the definition of the root vectors of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
. We adapt the construction in
[
BGKNR12
]
to our choice of coproduct, recall the relation (
2.33
).
Let us set

(B.1)

e_{\alpha}=E_{1}K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad e_{\delta-\alpha}=E_{0}K_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,\qquad f_{\alpha}=K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{1}\ ,\qquad f_{\delta-\alpha}=K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{0}\ .

The other root vectors are defined by the recursion relations:

(B.2)

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{k\delta}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}[e_{\alpha+(k-1)\delta},e_{\delta-\alpha}]_{q}\ ,

\displaystyle e_{\alpha+k\delta}

\displaystyle=[2]_{q}^{-1}[e_{\alpha+(k-1)\delta},e^{\prime}_{\delta}]\ ,

\displaystyle e_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta}

\displaystyle=[2]_{q}^{-1}[e^{\prime}_{\delta},e_{\delta-\alpha+(k-1)\delta}]\ ,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{k\delta}

\displaystyle=q[f_{\delta-\alpha},f_{\alpha+(k-1)\delta}]_{q^{-1}}\ ,

\displaystyle f_{\alpha+k\delta}

\displaystyle=[2]_{q}^{-1}[f^{\prime}_{\delta},f_{\alpha+(k-1)\delta}]\ ,

\displaystyle f_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta}

\displaystyle=[2]_{q}^{-1}[f_{\delta-\alpha+(k-1)\delta},f^{\prime}_{\delta}]\ ,\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\ .

The root vectors
e_{k\delta}
,
f_{k\delta}
are defined via the generating functions

\displaystyle(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}e_{k\delta}z^{-k}

\displaystyle=\log\left(1+(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}e^{\prime}_{k\delta}z^{-k}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle-(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}f_{k\delta}z^{-k}

\displaystyle=\log\left(1-(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}f^{\prime}_{k\delta}z^{-k}\right)\ .

B.2.
Khoroshkin-Tolstoy construction

Let
\{\alpha+k\delta\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\cup\{k\delta\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\cup\{\delta-\alpha+k\delta\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be the positive root system of
\widehat{sl_{2}}
. We choose the root ordering as

(B.3)

\alpha,\ \alpha+\delta,\ \ldots,\ \alpha+k\delta,\ \ldots,\delta,\ 2\delta,\ \ldots,\ \ell\delta,\ \ldots,\ \ldots,\ (\delta-\alpha)+m\delta,\ldots,\ (\delta-\alpha)+\delta,\ \delta-\alpha\ ,

for any
k
,
\ell
,
m

\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, the universal R-matrix obtained by Khoroshkin and Tolstoy takes the following factorized form

(B.4)

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{+}\mathcal{R}^{0}\mathcal{R}^{-}q^{\frac{1}{2}h_{1}\otimes h_{1}}\ ,

where
20
20

20

Here we use the notation

\displaystyle{\prod^{\overleftarrow{n}}_{k=0}}a(k)=a(n)a(n-1)\ldots a(0)
and
\displaystyle{\prod^{\overrightarrow{n}}_{k=0}}a(k)=a(0)a(1)\ldots a(n)
, for any function
a(n)
.

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{+}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\prod^{\overrightarrow{\infty}}_{k=0}}\exp_{q^{-2}}\left((q-q^{-1})e_{\alpha+k\delta}\otimes f_{\alpha+k\delta}\right)\ ,

(B.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{0}

\displaystyle=\exp\left((q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{k}{[2k]_{q}}e_{k\delta}\otimes f_{k\delta}\right)\ ,

(B.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\prod^{\overleftarrow{\infty}}_{k=0}}\exp_{q^{-2}}\left((q-q^{-1})e_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta}\otimes f_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta}\right)\ ,

with
q^{h_{1}}\equiv K_{1}
and the
q
-exponential is

(B.8)

\exp_{q}(x)=1+\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{x^{k}}{(k)_{q}!}\ ,\quad(k)_{q}!=(1)_{q}(2)_{q}\cdots(k)_{q},\;(k)_{q}=\frac{q^{k}-1}{q-1}\ .

We also notice that
[e_{k\delta},e_{\ell\delta}]=0=[f_{k\delta},f_{\ell\delta}]
for any
k,\ell
, and so the exponent in (
B.6
) can be also written in the form of semi-infinite product of exponents involving only one term
\sim

e_{k\delta}\otimes f_{k\delta}
.

B.3.
Evaluation of the universal R-matrix.

In the previous subsection, the explicit form of the universal R-matrix was recalled. It is expressed as a product of
q
-exponentials with root vectors in the arguments. Now, we evaluate the second tensor product component of the universal R-matrix by taking its image under the formal fundamental evaluation representation as introduced in Section
3.5
.

B.3.1.
Evaluation of the root vectors

The action of the evaluation map defined in (
2.40
) on the first root vectors gives

(B.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e_{\delta-\alpha})

\displaystyle=u^{-1}FK^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=u^{-1}EK^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(K_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=K^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

(B.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f_{\delta-\alpha})

\displaystyle=uq^{-1}EK^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=uq^{-1}FK^{\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=K^{\frac{1}{2}}\ .

The image of the other root vectors of
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}
in (
B.2
) under the evaluation map are obtained by induction similarly to
[
BGKNR12
, Sect. 4.4]
. They are given for
k\in\mathbb{N}
by:

(B.11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e_{\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{-2k-1}q^{-k}EK^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{-2k-1}q^{k}FK^{-k+\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f_{\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{2k+1}q^{-k-1}FK^{k+\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{2k+1}q^{k-1}EK^{k-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

and for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}

(B.12)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e^{\prime}_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{-2k}[E,F]_{q^{k}}K^{-k+1}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(e_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}u^{-2k}}{(q-q^{-1})k}(C_{k}-(q^{k}+q^{-k})K^{-k})\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f^{\prime}_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{2k}[E,F]_{q^{-k}}K^{k-1}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{u}(f_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}u^{2k}}{(q-q^{-1})k}(C_{k}-(q^{k}+q^{-k})K^{k})\ ,

where the elements
C_{k}
are defined by the generating function

(B.13)

\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}(-1)^{k-1}C_{k}\frac{z^{-k}}{k}=\log(1+Cz^{-1}+z^{-2})\ ,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\ ,

and where
C
is the central element of
U_{q}sl_{2}
given in (
A.5
). For instance by expanding (
B.13
) we get the first elements of
C_{k}

(B.14)

C_{1}=C\ ,\qquad C_{2}=C^{2}-2\ ,\qquad C_{3}=C^{3}-3C\ ,\qquad C_{4}=C^{4}-4C^{2}+2\ .

Recall
E_{ab}
is the matrix with zero everywhere except 1 in the entry
(a,b)
. The matrix multiplication obeys

(B.15)

E_{ab}E_{cd}=\delta_{b,c}E_{ad}\ .

In this notation, the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
finite-dimensional representation of
U_{q}sl_{2}
reads

(B.16)

\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(K^{m})=q^{m}E_{11}+q^{-m}E_{22}\ ,\qquad\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(E)=E_{12}\ ,\qquad\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(F)=E_{21}\ ,

and the central elements
C_{k}
become, for all
k\geq 1
,

(B.17)

\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}(C_{k})=(q^{2k}+q^{-2k})\mathbb{I}_{2}\ .

In order to obtain Ding-Frenkel L-operators and R-matrices from the universal R-matrix, one also needs the image of the root vectors under the representation map
\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}\colon\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}\rightarrow\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, which is given by:

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(e_{\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{-2k-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{12}\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(e_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{-2k-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{21}\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(f_{\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{2k+1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}E_{21}\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(f_{\delta-\alpha+k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}u^{2k+1}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}E_{12}\ ,

(B.18)

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(e^{\prime}_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{-2k}(qE_{11}-q^{-1}E_{22})\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(e_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{-2k}\frac{[k]_{q}}{k}(q^{k}E_{11}-q^{-k}E_{22})\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(f^{\prime}_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{2k}(q^{-1}E_{11}-qE_{22})\ ,

\displaystyle\pi_{u}^{\frac{1}{2}}(f_{k\delta})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}u^{2k}\frac{[k]_{q}}{k}(q^{-k}E_{11}-q^{k}E_{22})\ .

Recall that
{\bf L}^{\pm}(u)
are defined in (
4.74
).
We now compute explicitly
\mathbf{L}^{+}(u)
and
[\mathbf{L}^{-}(u^{-1})]^{-1}
.

B.3.2.
\mathbf{L}^{+}(u)

Recall the factorized form of the universal R-matrix (
B.4
). We now compute the image of
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}
,
\mathfrak{R}^{0}
,
q^{\frac{1}{2}h_{1}\otimes h_{1}}
under the action of
({\text{id}}\otimes\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u^{-1}})
.
First, from (
B.5
) and with (
B.18
) we get:

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{+})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\prod^{\overrightarrow{\infty}}_{k=0}}\exp_{q^{-2}}\left((-u^{-2})^{k}u^{-1}(q-q^{-1})q^{-\frac{1}{2}}e_{\alpha+k\delta}\otimes E_{21}\right)

(B.19)

\displaystyle=1\otimes\mathbb{I}_{2}+e^{+}(u)\otimes E_{21}\ ,

where

(B.20)

e^{+}(u)=(q-q^{-1})q^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{-1}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}}(-u^{-2})^{k}e_{\alpha+k\delta}\right)\ .

Similarly, from (
B.7
) and with (
B.18
) we have:

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{-})

(B.21)

\displaystyle=1\otimes\mathbb{I}_{2}+f^{+}(u)\otimes E_{12}\ ,

where

(B.22)

A straightforward calculation from (
B.6
) and using (
B.18
) yields

(B.23)

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathfrak{R}^{0})=k^{+}(u)\otimes E_{11}+\tilde{k}^{+}(u)\otimes E_{22}\ ,

where

(B.24)

k^{+}(u)=\exp\left(\!-(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{(-u^{-2}q^{-1})^{k}}{q^{k}+q^{-k}}e_{k\delta}\right),\;\tilde{k}^{+}(u)=\exp\left(\!(q-q^{-1})\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{(-u^{-2}q)^{k}}{q^{k}+q^{-k}}e_{k\delta}\right).

Then, using
\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}_{u}(h_{1})=E_{11}-E_{22}
, we get

(B.25)

\displaystyle({\text{id}}\otimes\pi_{u^{-1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})(q^{\frac{1}{2}h_{1}\otimes h_{1}})=K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes E_{11}+K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\otimes E_{22}\ .\

Finally, combining (
B.19
)-(
B.25
), we get

(B.26)

e^{+}(u)k^{+}(u)K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&\;\tilde{k}^{+}(u)K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+e^{+}(u)k^{+}(u)f^{+}(u)K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Note that from the definition of
e^{+}(u)
,

k^{+}(u)
,
\tilde{k}^{+}(u)
in (

), (
B.22
), (

is a formal power series in
u^{-1}
, i.e.

is in
\mathcal{L}U_{q}sl_{2}[[u^{-1}]]\otimes\text{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

B.4.
The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator
\mathbf{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)

We now compute the spin-
\frac{1}{2}

{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
defined in (
4.1
). It is obtained by taking the image of
{\bf L}^{+}(u)
under the evaluation with
(\mathsf{ev}_{v}\otimes{\text{id}})
.

Recall the expression of
{\bf L}^{+}(u)
in (
B.26
). The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
L-operator is then obtained by evaluating
e^{+}(u)
,
f^{+}(u)
,
k^{+}(u)
,
\tilde{k}^{+}(u)
defined in (
B.20
), (
B.22
), (

Let us first introduce the function
[
BGKNR12
]

(B.34)

\Lambda(u)=\displaystyle{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}}\frac{C_{k}}{(q^{k}+q^{-k})}\frac{u^{k}}{k}\ ,

where the central elements
C_{k}
are defined by (
B.13
). Note that it satisfies

(B.35)

\Lambda(uq)+\Lambda(uq^{-1})=-\log(1-Cu+u^{2})\ .

From the evaluated root vectors (
B.9
) and (

), we get:

(B.36)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(e^{+}(u))

\displaystyle=(q-q^{-1})q^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{-1}v^{-1}EK^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1}K^{-1}\right)^{-1}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(f^{+}(u))

\displaystyle=(q-q^{-1})q^{-\frac{1}{2}}u^{-1}v^{-1}FK^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}qK^{-1}\right)^{-1}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(k^{+}(u))

\displaystyle=e^{\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1})}\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1}K^{-1}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(\tilde{k}^{+}(u))

\displaystyle=e^{-\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q)}\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}qK^{-1}\right)^{-1}\ .

For instance, let us now compute the evaluation of the matrix entry
(2,2)
of
{\bf L}^{+}(u)
in (
B.26
), it reads:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(({\bf L}^{+}(u))_{22})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ev}_{v}\Big(\tilde{k}^{+}(u)K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+e^{+}(u)k^{+}(u)f^{+}(u)K_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(e^{-\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q)}+e^{\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1})}u^{-2}v^{-2}(q-q^{-1})^{2}EF\Big)\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}qK^{-1}\right)^{-1}K^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1})}\Big(1+u^{-4}v^{-4}-u^{-2}v^{-2}(C-(q-q^{-1})^{2}EF)\Big)\left(1-u^{-2}v^{-2}qK^{-1}\right)^{-1}K^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ ,

where we used (
B.35
) on the third line, and where
C
is defined in (
A.5
). Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{ev}_{v}(({\bf L}^{+}(u))_{22})

\displaystyle=e^{\Lambda(u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1})}\left(K^{-\frac{1}{2}}-u^{-2}v^{-2}q^{-1}K^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\ .

Computing the other entries, we have

(B.37)

{\bf L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uv)=(\mathsf{ev}_{v}\otimes{\text{id}})({\bf L}^{+}(u))=\mu(uv)\mathcal{L}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(uv)\ ,

where

and

Similarly, evaluating the Ding-Frenkel L-operator in (

Appendix C
Ordering relations for
\mathcal{A}_{q}

Lemma C.1
.

The following relations hold in
{\cal A}_{q}[[u^{-1}]]
:

(C.1)

\displaystyle\cal G_{-}(v)\cal G_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\cal G_{+}(u)\cal G_{-}(v)+\rho(q^{2}-q^{-2})\Big(\cal W_{+}(u)\cal W_{+}(v)-\cal W_{-}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-UV}{U-V}(\cal W_{+}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)-\cal W_{+}(v)\cal W_{-}(u))\Big)\ ,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\cal W_{-}(v)\cal W_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\cal W_{+}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)+\frac{1}{V-U}\Big(\frac{(q-q^{-1})}{\rho(q+q^{-1})}\big(\cal G_{+}(u)\cal G_{-}(v)-\cal G_{+}(v)\cal G_{-}(u)\big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}\big(\cal G_{+}(u)-\cal G_{-}(u)+\cal G_{-}(v)-\cal G_{+}(v)\big)\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\cal W_{-}(v)\cal G_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{U-V}\Big(\big(Uq^{-1}-Vq\big)\cal G_{+}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)-(q-q^{-1})\Big(\cal W_{+}(u)\cal G_{+}(v)

(C.3)

\displaystyle-\cal W_{+}(v)\cal G_{+}(u)-U\cal G_{+}(v)\cal W_{-}(u)\Big)+\rho\big(U\cal W_{-}(u)-V\cal W_{-}(v)-\cal W_{+}(u)+\cal W_{+}(v)\big)\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\cal W_{-}(v)\cal G_{-}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(V-U)}\Big(\big(Vq^{-1}-Uq\big)\cal G_{-}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)-(q-q^{-1})\big(\cal W_{+}(u)\cal G_{-}(v)-\cal W_{+}(v)\cal G_{-}(u)

(C.4)

\displaystyle-U\cal G_{-}(v)\cal W_{-}(u)\big)+\rho\big(U\cal W_{-}(u)-V\cal W_{-}(v)-\cal W_{+}(u)+\cal W_{+}(v)\big)\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\cal G_{+}(v)\cal W_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{U-V}\Big(\big(Uq-Vq^{-1}\big)\cal W_{+}(u)\cal G_{+}(v)-(q-q^{-1})\big(\cal G_{+}(v)\cal W_{-}(u)

(C.5)

\displaystyle+\rho\big(U\cal W_{+}(u)-V\cal W_{+}(v)-\cal W_{-}(u)+\cal W_{-}(v)\big)\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\cal G_{-}(v)\cal W_{+}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(V-U)}\Big(\big(Vq-Uq^{-1}\big)\cal W_{+}(u)\cal G_{-}(v)-(q-q^{-1})\big(\cal G_{-}(v)\cal W_{-}(u)-\cal G_{-}(u)\cal W_{-}(v)

(C.6)

\displaystyle+V\cal W_{+}(v)\cal G_{-}(u)\big)+\rho\big(U\cal W_{+}(u)-V\cal W_{+}(v)-\cal W_{-}(u)+\cal W_{-}(v)\big)\Big)\ .

Proof.

The first two ordering relations (
C.1
), (
C.2
), are obtained directly from (
5.9
) and (
5.10
). The third relation (
C.3
) follows from (
5.11
) and (
5.13
) by replacing the element which is not in the chosen order. The relations (
C.4
)–(
C.6
) are derived similarly.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
5.7

For
(j_{1},j_{2})=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, the reflection equation (
5.30
) holds for
\mathcal{K}^{(\frac{1}{2})}(u)
due to
[
BS09
]
.
The proof is divided into three parts:
We first show the case
(j_{1},j_{2})=(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})
, assuming the equation (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(\frac{1}{2},j)
. Then, we prove the case
(j+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, assuming (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(j,\frac{1}{2})
.
Finally, the generic case (
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
) is proven considering two distinct cases
j_{1}\geq j_{2}
and
j_{1}\leq j_{2}
, using
that the reflection equation (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(j+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
and
(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})
.

First of all, the following Yang-Baxter equation holds for any
j_{1}
,
j_{2}

\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
:

(D.1)

R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})R_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{3})}(u_{1}/u_{3})R_{23}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2}/u_{3})=R_{23}^{(j_{2},j_{3})}(u_{2}/u_{3})R_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{3})}(u_{1}/u_{3})R_{12}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u_{1}/u_{2})\ .

This is due to the fact that
\mathcal{R}^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
is proportional to
R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)
, see (
4.39
), and it satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation in (
4.11
).
In what follows we use the shorthand notation

(D.2)

R_{k\ell}=R_{k\ell}^{(j_{k},j_{\ell})}(u_{k}/u_{\ell})\ ,\quad\bar{R}_{k\ell}=R_{k\ell}^{(j_{k},j_{\ell})}(u_{\ell}/u_{k})\ ,\quad\hat{R}_{k\ell}=R_{k\ell}^{(j_{k},j_{\ell})}(u_{k}u_{\ell})\ ,\quad{\cal K}_{\ell}={\cal K}_{\ell}^{(j_{\ell})}(u_{\ell})\ ,

and denote by
R_{\langle\ell\;\ell+1\rangle k}^{(j+\frac{1}{2},j_{k})}(u)
,
R_{k\langle\ell\;\ell+1\rangle}^{(j_{k},j+\frac{1}{2})}(u)
,
{\cal K}^{(j+\frac{1}{2})}_{\langle\ell\;\ell+1\rangle}(u)
the resulting R-matrices and K-operators obtained by fusing the space at position
\ell
with the one at position
\ell+1
, for
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

According to the notation (
D.2
), the Yang-Baxter equation (
D.1
) reads

(D.3)

\displaystyle R_{12}R_{13}R_{23}=R_{23}R_{13}R_{12}\ ,\;\;\text{or}\;\;R_{12}\hat{R}_{13}\hat{R}_{23}

\displaystyle=\hat{R}_{23}\hat{R}_{13}R_{12}\ ,\;\;\text{or}\;\;\hat{R}_{12}\hat{R}_{13}\bar{R}_{23}=\bar{R}_{23}\hat{R}_{13}\hat{R}_{12}\ ,

where the first two relations in (
D.3
) are related by
u_{3}\rightarrow u_{3}^{-1}
, and the last two relations in (
D.3
) are related by
u_{2}\rightarrow u_{2}^{-1}
. In the following, we will need relations derived from the Yang-Baxter equation:

(D.4)

\hat{R}_{13}\hat{R}_{12}R_{23}=R_{23}\hat{R}_{12}\hat{R}_{13}\ ,\quad R_{13}\hat{R}_{12}\hat{R}_{23}=\hat{R}_{23}\hat{R}_{12}R_{13}\ .

The first equality
is obtained from the first relation in (
D.3
) by multiplying both sides on the left and on the right by
\bar{R}_{23}
, using the unitarity property of the R-matrix in (
4.55
), that is
R_{23}\bar{R}_{23}\propto\mathbb{I}
, and substituting
u_{2}\rightarrow u_{2}^{-1}
,
u_{3}\rightarrow u_{3}^{-1}
. The two equations in (
D.4
) are related by
u_{3}\rightarrow u_{3}^{-1}
.

First, we give a relation derived from the reflection equation that will be used later.

Lemma D.1
.

Assuming the reflection equation holds for any
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
,
j_{3}
, then

(D.5)

\mathcal{K}_{1}\hat{R}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{2}\bar{R}_{12}=\bar{R}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{2}\hat{R}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{1}\ .

Proof.

According to the notation (
D.2
), the reflection equation (
5.30
) reads

(D.6)

R_{12}\mathcal{K}_{1}\hat{R}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{2}=\mathcal{K}_{2}\hat{R}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{1}R_{12}\ .

The equation (
D.5
) is obtained by multiplying on both sides of (
D.6
) on the left and on the right by
\bar{R}_{12}
and using the unitarity property (
4.55
).
∎

In the following, we will need various expressions of the fused R-matrices.

.

The fused R-matrices defined in (

) satisfy the following relations:

(D.7)

\displaystyle R^{(j_{1},j_{2})}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{1})}_{\langle 12\rangle}R_{23}^{(j_{1}-\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})R_{13}^{(\frac{1}{2},j_{2})}(uq^{j_{1}-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j_{1})}_{\langle 12\rangle}\ ,

(D.8)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}R_{12}^{(j_{1},\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-j_{2}+\frac{1}{2}})R_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j_{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\ ,

(D.9)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{(j_{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}R_{13}^{(j_{1},j_{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{-\frac{1}{2}})R_{12}^{(j_{1},\frac{1}{2})}(uq^{j_{2}-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{E}^{(j_{2})}_{\langle 23\rangle}\ .

Proof.

First, note that the r.h.s. of (
D.7
)-(
D.8
) are directly derived from the expressions for the R-matrices in (

) and (
4.46
), while the r.h.s. of (

21

The relation (

but it can be generalized to any

in (

).
of (

Thus, we have

.
Then, inserting in the r.h.s. of the latter relation the corresponding R-matrices, the equations (

Finally, it is easily checked that they indeed hold using (

Proof of Theorem
5.7
.

We are now ready to show that the reflection equation (
5.30
) is satisfied by the fused K-operator (
5.29
) for all
j_{1}
,
j_{2}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
We consider separately (
5.30
) for two distinct cases
j_{1}\geq j_{2}
and
j_{1}\leq j_{2}
:

(i)

(j_{1},j_{2})=(j+\frac{1}{2},k+\frac{1}{2})
with
0\leq k\leq j
.

(ii)

(j_{1},j_{2})=(\ell+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})
with
0\leq\ell\leq j
.

The first case is shown by induction on
k
. For
k=0
, the equation (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(j+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
due to Lemma
D.4
. Now, assume (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(j+\frac{1}{2},k)
with a fixed value of
k\leq j
. The case
(i)
is shown similarly to the proof of Lemma
D.3
.
Indeed, fix in (
D.12
)
j_{1}=j+\frac{1}{2}
,
j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
,
j_{3}=k
and
u_{1}=u
,
u_{2}=vq^{-k}
,
u_{3}=vq^{\frac{1}{2}}
(instead of (
D.13
)). Then, the rest of the proof is the same as for Lemma
D.3
, using now (
D.11
), (
D.14
), (
4.39
) and our assumption.

The second case is also shown by induction on
\ell
. For
\ell=0
, the equation (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2})
due to Lemma
D.3
. Assuming that (
5.30
) holds for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(\ell,j+\frac{1}{2})
with a fixed value of
\ell\leq j
, then the proof of the case
(ii)
is similar to Lemma
D.4
. Fix in (
D.15
)
j_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
,
j_{2}=\ell
,
j_{3}=j+\frac{1}{2}
and
u_{1}=uq^{-\ell}
,
u_{2}=uq^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
u_{3}=v
(instead of (
D.16
)). Then, the rest of the proof is the same as for Lemma
D.4
, using now (
D.11
), (
D.14
), (
4.39
) and our assumption.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.7
.
∎
